# BIB111 — Old Testament Survey

## Course objectives

- 1. Discuss the importance, structure, authors, and canon of the Old Testament.
- 2. Explain how Genesis 1–11 serves as the introduction to the Old Testament, and summarize what it says about Creation, the Fall, the Flood, Noah and his sons, and Babel.
- 3. Evaluate God’s covenants with Abraham, Moses, and David, and tell how each covenant influenced the history, religious life, and political relationships of the Jews.
- 4. Describe the Exodus, the Covenant, the tabernacle, the offerings, priests, laws, and feasts.
- 5. Summarize events that happened at Sinai, Kadesh, Moab, and Mount Nebo (Pisgah).
- 6. Identify characteristics and themes from the five poetic and wisdom books.
- 7. Comment on key leaders and prophets of each period from Abraham to Malachi.
- 8. Summarize and apply two cross-cultural, timeless principles from each book of the Old Testament.

## 1 - Treasures from the Old Testament

### The Old Testament

#### 1.1.1 — Summarize key facts about the Old Testament.

The Significance of the Old Testament

Summarize key facts about the Old Testament.

People buy more Bibles than any other book in the world. Millions live by its message. Bible translators have put the Scriptures into more than 2,300 languages. Over 95 percent of the world’s people have access to at least part of the Bible in a language they know. As you study, remember that you are joining millions of others who love and obey God’s Word.

The Old Testament is accepted by more people than are all other religious writings combined. Jews, Muslims, and Christians all find their beginnings in the Old Testament. With this in mind, let us consider four common questions about this first section of the Bible.

What is the Old Testament?

We refer to the first thirty-nine books of the Bible as the Old Testament . The word testament comes from Latin and describes “a covenant , agreement, or will.” Both Hebrew and Greek have words that mean something quite similar (see the following table).

Roots of the Word Testament

Hebrew

Greek

Latin

English

berit (covenant) (Genesis 9:9; Numbers 14:44)

diatheke (covenant) (2 Corinthians 3:14; Hebrews 8:6)

testamentum (covenant or testament)

testament (covenant, agreement, will)

Figure 1.1

The Old Testament, then, records God’s covenant with Abraham and his descendants (that is, the Israelites). It also describes how every blessing and cursing Israel received resulted from God’s faithfulness and Israel’s unfaithfulness to the covenant.

The Old Testament shows that God’s people continually struggled to keep God’s covenant. As we continue to study the Old Testament, we will come to see its ongoing significance for us today.

Who wrote the Old Testament, and when?

The Old Testament is a part of the Bible , a term derived from Greek and Latin words for “book.” The Bible is the book above all other books in that it contains sixty-six books written by about forty authors over a period of approximately 1,500–1,600 years—yet all inspired by the same Spirit. That is, all the words of Scripture—both Old and New Testaments—were breathed by God (2 Timothy 3:16). God’s Holy Spirit inspired the authors of the Old and New Testament books to write what He wanted them to write (2 Peter 1:21). Throughout this course, we will explore what we can know about the authors God inspired to write each Old Testament book as well as the approximate date of the writing.

How did the books of the Old Testament get into our Bible?

We refer to the books from Genesis to Malachi as the canon of the Old Testament. The word canon refers to the standard collection of sacred documents considered Scripture by the church. Thus, the Old Testament canon is the list of books that meet the standards for being in God’s Word. The thirty-nine books in our Old Testament are the same books that Jesus and the Jews of His day accepted as God’s Word.

The Catholic Bible and some Eastern Orthodox Bibles also contain books that are grouped in a class called the Apocrypha . The word Apocrypha first meant “hidden books” but later came to mean “not in the canon or list.” Although many non-Catholics are unaware of these books, they were never “hidden.” Instead, they were considered a part of the Catholic and Eastern Orthodox Bibles from the earliest times. They are found in ancient manuscripts that contain all the other books of the Bible. During the time of Martin Luther, the Catholics accepted seven apocryphal books as well as minor additions to the Books of Esther and Daniel (Harris 1969, 180). In response to the Protestant Reformation , the Catholic Church formally defined its canon in 1546 at the Council of Trent. Prior to this, the Catholic Church had unofficially maintained the same list of books for centuries without adding or removing any books.

Books of the Apocrypha Related to the Old Testament

1 Esdras (also known as 3 Esdras and Greek Ezra)

2 Esdras (also known as 4 Esdras and the Apocalypse of Ezra)

Tobit

Judith

Additions to Esther

Wisdom of Solomon

Ecclesiasticus (Sirach)

Baruch

Letter of Jeremiah

Prayer of Azariah and Song of the Three Young Men

Susanna

Bel and the Dragon

Prayer of Manasseh

1 Maccabees

2 Maccabees

Jerome’s List of Books in the Apocrypha ( New English Bible with Apocrypha 1976, iii)

Figure 1.2

Although these books were included in the various editions of the Septuagint (the Greek translation of the Hebrew Scriptures), they do not appear to have been originally written in Hebrew, and were not part of the Hebrew Scriptures during Christ’s time. Neither Jesus nor the apostles quoted any of the apocryphal books as Scripture (Harris 1969, 182–183). While the books in the Apocrypha are beneficial to read for acquiring background information on the Old and New Testaments, they do not meet the standards set for recognizing Scripture.

Why should we study the Old Testament?

We should study the Old Testament for at least three reasons. First, the Old Testament comprises approximately 75 percent of the entire Bible. To ignore it would be to miss a large portion of God-inspired Scripture! All of God’s Word “is a lamp to my feet and a light for my path” (Psalm 119:105). All of God’s Word is precious to us.

Second , God has not changed since Old Testament days. Some people see God as a God of anger and judgment in the Old Testament and a God of love in the New Testament. However, the Old Testament describes the God of justice and love (Deuteronomy 4–6; Jeremiah 9:23–24). He is the same God in both the Old and New Testaments. He has not changed. The apostle Paul knew the Old Testament well, and he referred to God as “the Father of compassion” (2 Corinthians 1:3). Additionally, the Early Church considered the rejection of the Old Testament to be heresy. We should therefore study the Old Testament to understand God better.

Third, the Old Testament is the seed and plant from which the fruit of the New Testament grew (Archer 1978, 17). As a result, the Old Testament provides the background we need to understand the New Testament. For example, John the Baptist called Jesus “the Lamb of God, who takes away the sin of the world” (John 1:29). We can understand the full significance of the term Lamb of God as we read the Old Testament and its descriptions of the animal sacrifices.

Think of all the Old Testament people mentioned in the New Testament. Without the Old Testament, we would not know much about Adam, Abel, Noah, Abraham, Moses, or others that Jesus and the apostles often referred to. The New Testament refers to the Old Testament more than six hundred times. Thus, we must know the Old Testament well to understand the New Testament.

List the major divisions of the Old Testament.

The Divisions of the Old Testament

The Jews of old, like Jews today, divided their Scriptures into three parts: the Law, the Prophets, and the Writings (Schultz 2000, 2).

The Law, also called the Pentateuch , included the five books of Moses: Genesis, Exodus, Leviticus, Numbers, and Deuteronomy.

The Prophets included eight books divided into two parts. First were the four books of the Former Prophets: Joshua, Judges, 1 and 2 Samuel (as one book), and 1 and 2 Kings (as one book). Then came the Latter Prophets: Isaiah, Jeremiah, Ezekiel, and the twelve Minor Prophets (as one book).

The Writings included eleven books: Psalms, Job, Proverbs, Ruth, Song of Songs, Ecclesiastes, Lamentations, Esther, Daniel, Ezra-Nehemiah (as one book), and 1 and 2 Chronicles (as one book) (Douglas 1978, 193).

Because the original Hebrew Scriptures were written on scrolls, the order was not of primary importance. It is helpful to see, however, that some books we commonly associate with history are also considered prophetic books in the Jewish tradition. However, as books replaced scrolls, the Scriptures began to be arranged in a certain order, like our modern Bibles (Schultz 2000, 2). The Septuagint (the Greek translation of the Old Testament) often arranged the books into five sections. Most Christian Bibles follow this order. For our study, we will divide the books of the Old Testament into five groups: (1) The Law (or Pentateuch), (2) History, (3) Poetry and Wisdom, (4) Major Prophets, and (5) Minor Prophets. The books in each major group are listed in the following chart.

The Old Testament

The Law (or Pentateuch)

History

Poetry and Wisdom

Major Prophets

Minor Prophets

Genesis Exodus Leviticus Numbers Deuteronomy

Joshua Judges Ruth 1 Samuel 2 Samuel 1 Kings 2 Kings 1 Chronicles 2 Chronicles Ezra Nehemiah Esther

Job Psalms Proverbs Ecclesiastes Song of Songs

Isaiah Jeremiah Lamentations Ezekiel Daniel

Hosea Joel Amos Obadiah Jonah Micah Nahum Habakkuk Zephaniah Haggai Zechariah Malachi

Figure 1.3

**Q:** Briefly describe the Old Testament.

**A:** It is the first thirty-nine books of the Bible. The word testament means “covenant, agreement, or will.”

**Q:** Explain what is meant by the term canon .

**A:** The word canon means “standard” and has come to mean “list.” In the Old Testament, the canon is the list of books that meet the standards for being in God’s Word.

**Q:** Why is it important to study the Old Testament?

**A:** Among the number of reasons are the following: the Old Testament is 75 percent of the entire Bible; God has not changed since Old Testament days, and we need to understand God better; and the Old Testament is the seed and plant from which the fruit of the New Testament grew.
