### The Beginnings (Genesis 1–11)

#### 1.2.1 — Discuss authorship issues regarding the Pentateuch.

The first Hebrew word in the Book of Genesis means “in the beginning.” The English title, “Genesis,” comes from the Greek translation of this word and means “origin.” Thus, the Book of Genesis is about the origin of creation. Its first eleven chapters describe Creation, the Fall, and the Flood. While we may want more information about these events, God inspired this section of Scripture to emphasize what He deems most important.

The stories recorded in these opening chapters of the Bible prove to be foundational for everything that comes after. In Genesis 1–11, we encounter the problem of sin and God’s plan to address it through the Redeemer. By the time we arrive at Genesis 12, we are ready to see how God establishes His covenant with the patriarchs in Genesis 12–50.

Since this is a survey course, we cannot cover every section of the Old Testament as thoroughly as we might like. Even so, the stories in Genesis are so pivotal for the biblical story that we will devote the first two chapters to the study of Genesis.

Discuss authorship issues regarding the Pentateuch.

Authorship

As noted in the previous lesson, the first five books of the Bible are called the Torah or the Pentateuch. The Hebrew word torah means law or instruction. In Greek, pente means “five,” and teuchos means “scroll” or “book.” Thus, Pentateuch means “five books.” It includes Genesis, Exodus, Leviticus, Numbers, and Deuteronomy.

Who wrote the first five books of the Bible? This question raises serious issues and can be a challenging topic for some people to consider. At the most foundational level, God is the author. Through the Holy Spirit, He inspired the composition of Genesis through Deuteronomy (and the rest of the Bible). But what human did God’s Spirit inspire to write these books? Strictly speaking, the author of Genesis through Deuteronomy never identifies himself. In this sense, the books are anonymous. Even so, Jewish and Christian traditions have historically credited Moses as the author of these books.

Not everyone agrees that Moses wrote all of the Pentateuch, however. People who adopt this position point to several pieces of information. First, the final section of Deuteronomy references Moses’s death and is written from a later perspective (Deuteronomy 34:1–12). Second, some references appear to require a later date. For example, Genesis 21:32 references the Philistines, but the Philistines did not come to the land of Israel until much later in history. Third, many of the stories in Genesis originated centuries before Moses’s time. For these reasons and more, some people reject Mosaic authorship completely.

Many people, however, still believe that Moses wrote the Pentateuch. Their arguments rely, in part, on the weight of tradition. For the vast majority of history, the Jewish and Christian traditions held to Mosaic authorship. Additionally, while nothing in the Pentateuch ever attributes the whole collection to Moses, there are numerous references to Moses’s writing activities. For example, “God commands him to record certain historical events (Ex. 17:14; Num. 33:2) and laws (Ex. 24:4; 34:27) as well as a song (Deut. 31:22; see Deut. 32)” (Longman 1994, 41).

Some people prefer to combine aspects of the different arguments. For example, Moses must have used ancient sources for many of the stories in the Book of Genesis. He likely relied on oral tradition and perhaps some written material to compose the first book of the Bible. Additionally, passages like the one at the end of Deuteronomy suggest that someone or some people edited the Pentateuch in the years following Moses’s life. Even so, the internal biblical evidence strongly suggests that Moses wrote the material found in the Pentateuch.

Tremper Longman III offers a helpful concluding summary:

The conservative view has always been qualified, however subtly, by admitting non-Mosaic elements to the Torah. While in the minds of most, these non-Mosaic parts are few and far between, it does indicate that to speak of Moses as author of the Pentateuch is not the same as saying that every word is the result of his work. Since there are what appear to be obvious later additions, many conservatives speak in terms of the “essential authorship” of Moses. This expression vigorously affirms Moses as the author of the Torah, while also leaving open the possibility of later canonical additions. (Longman 1994, 42)

Outline of Genesis 1–11

Genesis 1–11 is the introduction not only of the Pentateuch but also of the entire Old Testament. The New Testament quotes Genesis 1–11 at least sixty times (Horton 1995, 218). These first eleven chapters of the Bible explain humankind’s creation, fall, judgment, and scattering. Thus, they introduce God, humans, and the problem of sin.

As Genesis 1–2 teach, God created humanity in His image. Tragically, sin separated humans from God. Thankfully, God had a plan for our redemption. In Genesis 1–11, we see this in God’s prophecy about Eve (Genesis 3:15) and His preservation of humanity through Noah’s family (Genesis 6–9). While we will explore this theme of salvation and the Messiah throughout the Old Testament, it is important to see that Genesis 1–11 introduces the background and the problem that the entire Bible discusses.

I. The Creation Account (1–2)

A. Summary of all Creation (1:1–2:4)

B. Creation of Adam and Eve (2:4–25)

II. Humanity’s Fall and Its Results (3–5)

A. Adam and Eve’s Disobedience and Judgment (3)

B. Cain and the Ungodly Lineage (4:1–24)

C. Seth and the Godly Lineage (4:25–5:32)

III. The Flood: God’s Judgment (6:1–8:17)

IV. Humankind’s New Beginning (8:18–11:32)

A. God’s Covenant with Noah (8:20–9:17)

B. Noah and His Sons (9:18–10:32)

C. The Tower of Babel (11:1–9)

D. The Messianic Line of Shem (11:10–32)

Evaluate three viewpoints about Creation.

The Creation Account (Genesis 1–2)

The first two chapters of Genesis establish critical aspects of the Christian worldview. They explain that God intentionally created the universe and everything in it. These chapters also describe the origin and purpose of human existence. They do not provide us with a detailed, scientific explanation of creation. Instead, they simply presuppose that God created everything and emphasize the theological points God intended for us to learn.

Views Concerning Creation

While Genesis 1 – 2 should not be read as scientific accounts of creation, many people still wonder how these stories relate to the origin of the universe. Over the years, scholars have offered several proposals.

The Gap Theory (1:1–31)

Supporters of the gap theory think that a time gap occurred between the first two verses of Genesis. That is, they believe Genesis 1:1 shows God’s first creation, which was afterward destroyed by God’s judgment. Verse 2 then describes a dark, fallen creation many years later. Some refer to the prophecy against “the ruler of Tyre” in Ezekiel 28, linking the dark condition in Genesis 1:2 to the fall of Satan and his angels (Luke 10:18). According to the gap theory, Genesis 1:3–31 describes how God restored creation after Satan’s fall.

The Step-by-Step View (1:1–31)

This view states that Genesis 1:1–2 describes the first step God took in creation. That is, God created the heavens and earth like a lump of clay, ready for a potter to shape. Then, in Days 1–3, God formed the earth. In Days 4–6, He filled the earth (Horton 1995, 226–227).

Step or Act of Creation

Genesis

God created the heavens and earth, dark and empty.

1:1–2

Day 1: Light

1:3–5

Day 2: Air between heavens and earth

1:6–8

Day 3: Dry ground, plants, and trees

1:9–13

Day 4: Light-bearers (sun, moon, and stars)

1:14–19

Day 5: Fish and birds

1:20–23

Day 6: Animals and humans

1:24–31

Figure 1.4

Even within the step-by-step view, people have many different beliefs about how long God took to complete creation. While the Bible says the work of creation took six days, it does not explain the length of each day. As with other Scripture passages, the word day in Genesis 1–11 may mean a long period of time. Peter tells us that with the Lord, “a day is like a thousand years, and a thousand years are like a day” (2 Peter 3:8). On the other hand, a day in Scripture often refers to twenty-four hours (Genesis 8:12).

The Literary Framework View (1:1–31)

Some Bible interpreters understand the organization of the days in Genesis 1 to be topical and theological, not necessarily chronological or sequential (Moreland and Reynolds 1999, 138). This is called the literary framework view because of the literary structure of the biblical text. These interpreters view Days 4–6 as being parallel to Days 1–3. For example, Day 4 is parallel to Day 1 in that both days deal with the subject of light. Days 2 and 5 are similar in that waters and sky are the theme; yet in Day 5, the animals that inhabit these areas are also mentioned. The third and sixth days deal with land, although the creation of animals and humankind are mentioned in the account of the sixth day (Blocher 1984, 51).

The important thing in all of these views is that God is acknowledged as our Creator and Provider.

Genesis 1 and Other Ancient Near East Accounts

While hypotheses about the relationship between the creation of the world and the details of Genesis 1 remain interesting, they should not overshadow the theological emphases God inspired in the text. When one considers the creation account within its ancient Near Eastern (ANE) context, several important points become apparent.

First, God has no rival. Other creation accounts in the ancient Near East describe a cosmic battle as the origin of the universe. Sometimes, the god who creates the world uses the deceased corpse of another defeated deity to fashion creation. The God of the Bible stands unopposed at the beginning of the Bible. He does not need to battle anyone and He creates the universe by speaking it into existence (Longman 1994, 52).

Second, God creates humanity in His image. Other ancient Near Eastern accounts describe humanity as an afterthought or nuisance to the gods. The gods create humans to have them do menial tasks and free up more time for the gods to relax and engage in other activities. Genesis 1 describes humanity as the pinnacle of God’s creation. Humans are not an afterthought or a nuisance. They are the only aspect of creation God describes as “very good” (Genesis 1:31).

Creation of Humankind (2:4–25)

While Genesis 1–2:3 offers a 30,000-foot view of creation, Genesis 2:4–25 presents a more detailed account of God’s creative acts, especially as it relates to the creation of humanity. Contrary to some modern accounts of humanity’s origin, which often include a version of evolution, the Genesis account teaches us that God intentionally created humans. Humans did not manage to win the evolutionary battle.

Genesis 2:4–25 demonstrates God’s creation of and attentive care for humanity. First, God fashions the man out of the ground and breathes life into him. God names the man Adam, which comes from the Hebrew word for the “ground.” Next, the story describes the ground from which Adam was created as a paradise. It describes God as planting the garden and filling it with all sorts of trees for food. From the garden flows a river that splits into four other rivers that course through lands known for producing valuable metals.

After creating the garden, God placed Adam there because the garden needed someone to tend it. In doing so, God signaled the importance of work for humanity. From the very beginning, God designed humans for a purpose.

Finally, God recognizes that Adam should not be alone. The issue is not Adam’s loneliness, but a need for someone to assist him in tending the garden (Goldingay 2020, 62). So God creates a “helper” for Adam. The method of creation for this helper differs from Adam’s creation. God does not form Eve out of the ground but from one of Adam’s ribs. The similarity of the two creatures is even seen in the words used to describe them. In Hebrew, the word for man is ish , and the word for woman is isha . By the end of the chapter, humanity exists in harmony with creation and the Creator. This harmony is symbolized by the couple’s nakedness and lack of shame.

The Fall (Genesis 3–5)

Describe the Fall and the Flood.

Two life-altering events stand out in the history of humankind: (1) humanity’s fall in the Garden and (2) Christ’s coming to save fallen humanity. Even as the Book of Genesis records the account of the Fall , it anticipates the day when Christ would come to save humanity from sin. Therefore, the Book of Genesis plays a pivotal role in establishing a Christian worldview that includes the origin of humanity, the central problem humans face, and God’s plan to redeem His creation.

Adam and Eve (3:1–24)

The Scriptures show that the fall of humankind and its results are real. Adam and Eve were real people whose relationship to God depended on trust and obedience. However, they chose to disobey because they doubted God’s Word. They listened to the tempter and sinned by obeying his suggestions. Because of this sin, God judged the serpent, Satan, Eve, and Adam (Genesis 3:1–24; Romans 5:12, 15–19).

Yet God gave Adam and Eve mercy before judgment. How? He promised them that the Seed of the woman would defeat the seed of the serpent (Genesis 3:15). This was the first promise of the coming Messiah. Later in the Old Testament, God would give more details about the promised Messiah (Genesis 12:1–3; Numbers 24:17–19; 2 Samuel 7; 1 Chronicles 17:11–14; Isaiah 7:14; 9:6–7).

After showing mercy by promising Adam and Eve a Savior, God showed judgment by driving them out of the Garden. At that moment, they began to feel the full weight of the curse that sin brought. Still, in the midst of His judgment, God displayed His love by clothing them with animal skins.

Although God punished Adam and Eve for disobeying Him, He gave them hope for the future. When Cain was born, Eve expressed that hope by saying, “With the help of the Lord I have brought forth a man” (Genesis 4:1). She knew that, through the continuation of the human line, someday God’s promised Redeemer would crush the serpent’s head (3:15). From one generation to another, others held on to the hope of victory over sin and the serpent and thus kept the promise of redemption alive.

Cain (4:1–24)

However, Eve’s hope of a Redeemer would not be realized in Cain, who became the first murderer. Cain disobeyed by offering God a sacrifice that did not please Him (Genesis 4:3–5). It is not clear whether God instructed Cain and Abel about what type of sacrifice they should bring or why God was displeased with Cain’s offering. Although the biblical account acknowledges sin in Cain’s life (4:6–7), it does not specifically state what that sin is. In any case, the events surrounding the sacrifice resulted in Cain’s anger and jealousy, which led him to sin by murdering his brother (4:8; 1 John 3:12). God punished Cain by placing him under a curse and condemning him to wander the earth.

Genesis 4:17–24 includes a summary of Cain and his family tree, which may cover a long period of time. Some of Cain’s descendants raised flocks and herds. In time, they developed many useful things, including musical instruments. They learned how to forge metal such as bronze and iron into tools and weapons. Because of their progress, the people began to feel safe. They depended on themselves, not on God.

The Bible presents Lamech, a descendant of Cain, as a very proud man. He was the first man we know of to reject God’s plan of monogamy (Genesis 4:19). Also, he bragged about killing a man who hurt him. Lamech did not believe in an eye for an eye; he believed in a life for a bruise (4:23). Lamech considered himself ten times more valuable than Cain (4:24). This short story shows the expanding problem sin posed in the world after Adam and Eve’s initial disobedience.

Seth (4:25–5:32)

Adam and Eve renewed their hope in God when Seth was born. Eve said, “God has granted me another child in place of Abel, since Cain killed him” (Genesis 4:25). Seth’s legacy turned out to be very different from Cain’s.

As we examine Seth’s lineage, it is important to understand the approach of biblical cultures to compiling genealogies. The Hebrews used the word son much like we use the term descendant today. It indicated any relationship to an ancestor. Thus, a son (descendant) in the biblical text could refer to a son, grandson, great-grandson, and so on. As a result, the biblical genealogies are selective, not comprehensive. This allows genealogies to cover long periods of time, highlighting only key descendants. The genealogy of Genesis 5 covers a great deal of history, yet moves the narrative ahead quickly (Arnold 1998, 58).

With this in mind, we note that Seth’s descendants included Enosh (his son), Kenan, Mahalalel, Jared, Enoch, Methuselah, Lamech, and Noah. Several of those in Seth’s family line were godly men. In the days of Enosh, people “began to call on the name of the Lord” (Genesis 4:26).

Enoch was born several centuries after Seth and Enosh. He was a godly person, well known for his lifestyle of close fellowship with God. In the brief account of his life, the Bible twice emphasizes that Enoch walked with God (5:22, 24). He had such a close relationship with God that his life did not end in death. Rather, God raptured Enoch, taking him up to heaven while he was still alive.

The godly example of Seth’s line continued in Noah’s father, Lamech (a different Lamech than Cain’s descendant). The Scriptures record that Lamech believed and hoped in God. When Noah was born, Lamech prophesied, “He will comfort us in the labor and painful toil of our hands caused by the ground the Lord has cursed” (5:29). Lamech believed God would provide a way for people to be set free from the curse of sin that had expelled Adam and Eve from the Garden.

The Flood (Genesis 6:1–8:17)

While we commonly think of Genesis 3 as the story of the Fall, humanity’s descent into sin continues throughout much of Genesis 1–11. When readers arrive at Genesis 6:1, they discover just how pervasive the problem of sin had become. Humanity sinned against God so much that all of their thoughts were bent toward evil. Eight references to the earth in Genesis 6:5–13 emphasize the universal problem of sin (Hamilton 2005, 273). Human sinfulness was not confined to one location.

The story of the Flood shows that God does not react erratically to sin. Instead, He is heartbroken over humanity’s unfaithfulness (Genesis 6:6). God’s act of judgment against sin is characterized as an act of de-creation. Genesis 6:7 refers to the animals in ways that recall Genesis 1. Because humanity’s sin ruined God’s good creation, God systematically tore creation down so that He could renew it.

The attentive reader already knows that Noah will play a significant role in God’s plan. When Noah first appears in the genealogy of Genesis 5, his father predicts that Noah will help lift God’s curse from humanity (Genesis 5:29). Noah obeyed the Lord by building an ark to protect himself and his family. He housed both clean and unclean animals and stayed on the boat for approximately one year (compare Genesis 7:1–13 with 8:13–19) (Williams 2003b, 128). Noah does not fulfill his father’s prophecy in the way his father might have expected, but he does find favor in God’s eyes. Ultimately, his faithfulness to the Lord leads to humanity’s survival.

Although the Flood was a divine act of judgment, God also used the Flood to renew His covenant with humankind. We learn from this account that God tempers His judgment with His mercy. As “Noah found favor in the eyes of the Lord” (Genesis 6:8), people today can also be recipients of God’s grace.

A New Beginning (Genesis 8:18–11:32)

God’s Covenant with Noah (8:20–9:17)

God expressed His grace by giving people a brand new opportunity in the post-Flood world. When Noah left the ark, he worshipped God with an animal sacrifice. In response, God promised that He would never again destroy the human race and every living creature with a flood. The sign of the rainbow sealed His promise.

However, this did not mean that sin was now totally absent. Even as Noah presented his sacrifice after coming out of the ark, God acknowledged that “every inclination of the human heart is evil from childhood” (8:21). The Flood did not erase humankind’s sin nature. The sin nature would still express itself.

Noah and His Sons (9:18–10:32)

The sin nature’s continued existence is illustrated in the story of Ham, one of Noah’s sons. Ham failed to demonstrate respect for his father when he found Noah drunk and uncovered (9:20–22). As a result, Noah cursed Canaan, Ham’s son, saying that Canaan’s sons would be servants of servants. Some people have wrongly interpreted this event, attributing the slavery of African Americans to their descent from Canaan. Such a conclusion is wholly incorrect. The Canaanites were not the same color as African Americans. More importantly, the color of a person’s skin does not matter to God. He loves everyone and shows no favoritism.

Ham was the youngest of Noah’s three sons. His older brothers were Japheth and Shem. As noted in Genesis 10, each of Noah’s sons migrated to a different area: Japheth toward Spain and northern Asia Minor, Ham toward Africa (10:6–14), and Shem near the Persian Gulf (10:21–31).

The Tower of Babel (11:1–9)

After the Flood, the human race was one group that spoke only one language. Although God had originally directed Noah’s family to “fill the earth” (Genesis 9:1), many individuals settled on the Plain of Shinar for quite some time (11:1–2). As their pride and arrogance in their abilities and skills grew, they purposed to build a tower at Babel—a great tower that would reach to the heavens. The location of the tower is ironic because it would later develop into Babylon. In this way, the tower not only represents humanity’s temporary disobedience to God’s command, but it also symbolizes a destructive force throughout the history of God’s people. The Babylonians conquer Jerusalem in 587 and Babylon symbolizes opposition to God in the Book of Revelation.

Humanity’s main motivation was “so that we may make a name for ourselves” (11:4). However, God confused their language to hinder communication and cooperation and to halt their plans. As a result, people finally migrated to several different locations according to God’s original instructions.

The Messianic Line of Shem (11:10–32)

Genesis 11 ends with a synopsis of Shem’s lineage. The children of Shem are called “Shemites” (Semites), from which the word Semitic is derived. The list in Genesis 11 names ten families and ends with Terah, who moved from Ur to Haran. Terah was Abraham’s father. Through this nation, the promise of a Messiah would be fulfilled (Genesis 22:15–18; Matthew 1:1–2). Genesis 12–50 discusses the early patriarchs, whom we will study in the next chapter.

**Q:** Why is Moses considered to have written the Pentateuch?

**A:** In general, the Bible and all of Jewish and Christian tradition credit Moses as the author of the Pentateuch.

**Q:** Explain three views about creation.

**Q:** Give an example of God’s judgment and of His mercy in the Garden.

**A:** God punished Adam and Eve for disobeying Him by driving them out of the Garden. He showed them mercy by promising a Savior, the hope for the future.

**Q:** Compare Cain’s family record with Seth’s descendants.

**A:** God is not mentioned in Cain’s family tree; the people depended on themselves, not on God. Seth’s lineage included notable, godly men.

**Q:** Why are the Fall and the Flood significant Old Testament events?

**A:** Both events show that sin and evil existed, and because it was constantly increasing, God was sorry that He had created people. In both events, God’s judgment was tempered by His mercy and grace. 2 CHAPTER

#### 1.2.2 — Evaluate three viewpoints about Creation.

The first Hebrew word in the Book of Genesis means “in the beginning.” The English title, “Genesis,” comes from the Greek translation of this word and means “origin.” Thus, the Book of Genesis is about the origin of creation. Its first eleven chapters describe Creation, the Fall, and the Flood. While we may want more information about these events, God inspired this section of Scripture to emphasize what He deems most important.

The stories recorded in these opening chapters of the Bible prove to be foundational for everything that comes after. In Genesis 1–11, we encounter the problem of sin and God’s plan to address it through the Redeemer. By the time we arrive at Genesis 12, we are ready to see how God establishes His covenant with the patriarchs in Genesis 12–50.

Since this is a survey course, we cannot cover every section of the Old Testament as thoroughly as we might like. Even so, the stories in Genesis are so pivotal for the biblical story that we will devote the first two chapters to the study of Genesis.

Discuss authorship issues regarding the Pentateuch.

Authorship

As noted in the previous lesson, the first five books of the Bible are called the Torah or the Pentateuch. The Hebrew word torah means law or instruction. In Greek, pente means “five,” and teuchos means “scroll” or “book.” Thus, Pentateuch means “five books.” It includes Genesis, Exodus, Leviticus, Numbers, and Deuteronomy.

Who wrote the first five books of the Bible? This question raises serious issues and can be a challenging topic for some people to consider. At the most foundational level, God is the author. Through the Holy Spirit, He inspired the composition of Genesis through Deuteronomy (and the rest of the Bible). But what human did God’s Spirit inspire to write these books? Strictly speaking, the author of Genesis through Deuteronomy never identifies himself. In this sense, the books are anonymous. Even so, Jewish and Christian traditions have historically credited Moses as the author of these books.

Not everyone agrees that Moses wrote all of the Pentateuch, however. People who adopt this position point to several pieces of information. First, the final section of Deuteronomy references Moses’s death and is written from a later perspective (Deuteronomy 34:1–12). Second, some references appear to require a later date. For example, Genesis 21:32 references the Philistines, but the Philistines did not come to the land of Israel until much later in history. Third, many of the stories in Genesis originated centuries before Moses’s time. For these reasons and more, some people reject Mosaic authorship completely.

Many people, however, still believe that Moses wrote the Pentateuch. Their arguments rely, in part, on the weight of tradition. For the vast majority of history, the Jewish and Christian traditions held to Mosaic authorship. Additionally, while nothing in the Pentateuch ever attributes the whole collection to Moses, there are numerous references to Moses’s writing activities. For example, “God commands him to record certain historical events (Ex. 17:14; Num. 33:2) and laws (Ex. 24:4; 34:27) as well as a song (Deut. 31:22; see Deut. 32)” (Longman 1994, 41).

Some people prefer to combine aspects of the different arguments. For example, Moses must have used ancient sources for many of the stories in the Book of Genesis. He likely relied on oral tradition and perhaps some written material to compose the first book of the Bible. Additionally, passages like the one at the end of Deuteronomy suggest that someone or some people edited the Pentateuch in the years following Moses’s life. Even so, the internal biblical evidence strongly suggests that Moses wrote the material found in the Pentateuch.

Tremper Longman III offers a helpful concluding summary:

The conservative view has always been qualified, however subtly, by admitting non-Mosaic elements to the Torah. While in the minds of most, these non-Mosaic parts are few and far between, it does indicate that to speak of Moses as author of the Pentateuch is not the same as saying that every word is the result of his work. Since there are what appear to be obvious later additions, many conservatives speak in terms of the “essential authorship” of Moses. This expression vigorously affirms Moses as the author of the Torah, while also leaving open the possibility of later canonical additions. (Longman 1994, 42)

Outline of Genesis 1–11

Genesis 1–11 is the introduction not only of the Pentateuch but also of the entire Old Testament. The New Testament quotes Genesis 1–11 at least sixty times (Horton 1995, 218). These first eleven chapters of the Bible explain humankind’s creation, fall, judgment, and scattering. Thus, they introduce God, humans, and the problem of sin.

As Genesis 1–2 teach, God created humanity in His image. Tragically, sin separated humans from God. Thankfully, God had a plan for our redemption. In Genesis 1–11, we see this in God’s prophecy about Eve (Genesis 3:15) and His preservation of humanity through Noah’s family (Genesis 6–9). While we will explore this theme of salvation and the Messiah throughout the Old Testament, it is important to see that Genesis 1–11 introduces the background and the problem that the entire Bible discusses.

I. The Creation Account (1–2)

A. Summary of all Creation (1:1–2:4)

B. Creation of Adam and Eve (2:4–25)

II. Humanity’s Fall and Its Results (3–5)

A. Adam and Eve’s Disobedience and Judgment (3)

B. Cain and the Ungodly Lineage (4:1–24)

C. Seth and the Godly Lineage (4:25–5:32)

III. The Flood: God’s Judgment (6:1–8:17)

IV. Humankind’s New Beginning (8:18–11:32)

A. God’s Covenant with Noah (8:20–9:17)

B. Noah and His Sons (9:18–10:32)

C. The Tower of Babel (11:1–9)

D. The Messianic Line of Shem (11:10–32)

Evaluate three viewpoints about Creation.

The Creation Account (Genesis 1–2)

The first two chapters of Genesis establish critical aspects of the Christian worldview. They explain that God intentionally created the universe and everything in it. These chapters also describe the origin and purpose of human existence. They do not provide us with a detailed, scientific explanation of creation. Instead, they simply presuppose that God created everything and emphasize the theological points God intended for us to learn.

Views Concerning Creation

While Genesis 1 – 2 should not be read as scientific accounts of creation, many people still wonder how these stories relate to the origin of the universe. Over the years, scholars have offered several proposals.

The Gap Theory (1:1–31)

Supporters of the gap theory think that a time gap occurred between the first two verses of Genesis. That is, they believe Genesis 1:1 shows God’s first creation, which was afterward destroyed by God’s judgment. Verse 2 then describes a dark, fallen creation many years later. Some refer to the prophecy against “the ruler of Tyre” in Ezekiel 28, linking the dark condition in Genesis 1:2 to the fall of Satan and his angels (Luke 10:18). According to the gap theory, Genesis 1:3–31 describes how God restored creation after Satan’s fall.

The Step-by-Step View (1:1–31)

This view states that Genesis 1:1–2 describes the first step God took in creation. That is, God created the heavens and earth like a lump of clay, ready for a potter to shape. Then, in Days 1–3, God formed the earth. In Days 4–6, He filled the earth (Horton 1995, 226–227).

Step or Act of Creation

Genesis

God created the heavens and earth, dark and empty.

1:1–2

Day 1: Light

1:3–5

Day 2: Air between heavens and earth

1:6–8

Day 3: Dry ground, plants, and trees

1:9–13

Day 4: Light-bearers (sun, moon, and stars)

1:14–19

Day 5: Fish and birds

1:20–23

Day 6: Animals and humans

1:24–31

Figure 1.4

Even within the step-by-step view, people have many different beliefs about how long God took to complete creation. While the Bible says the work of creation took six days, it does not explain the length of each day. As with other Scripture passages, the word day in Genesis 1–11 may mean a long period of time. Peter tells us that with the Lord, “a day is like a thousand years, and a thousand years are like a day” (2 Peter 3:8). On the other hand, a day in Scripture often refers to twenty-four hours (Genesis 8:12).

The Literary Framework View (1:1–31)

Some Bible interpreters understand the organization of the days in Genesis 1 to be topical and theological, not necessarily chronological or sequential (Moreland and Reynolds 1999, 138). This is called the literary framework view because of the literary structure of the biblical text. These interpreters view Days 4–6 as being parallel to Days 1–3. For example, Day 4 is parallel to Day 1 in that both days deal with the subject of light. Days 2 and 5 are similar in that waters and sky are the theme; yet in Day 5, the animals that inhabit these areas are also mentioned. The third and sixth days deal with land, although the creation of animals and humankind are mentioned in the account of the sixth day (Blocher 1984, 51).

The important thing in all of these views is that God is acknowledged as our Creator and Provider.

Genesis 1 and Other Ancient Near East Accounts

While hypotheses about the relationship between the creation of the world and the details of Genesis 1 remain interesting, they should not overshadow the theological emphases God inspired in the text. When one considers the creation account within its ancient Near Eastern (ANE) context, several important points become apparent.

First, God has no rival. Other creation accounts in the ancient Near East describe a cosmic battle as the origin of the universe. Sometimes, the god who creates the world uses the deceased corpse of another defeated deity to fashion creation. The God of the Bible stands unopposed at the beginning of the Bible. He does not need to battle anyone and He creates the universe by speaking it into existence (Longman 1994, 52).

Second, God creates humanity in His image. Other ancient Near Eastern accounts describe humanity as an afterthought or nuisance to the gods. The gods create humans to have them do menial tasks and free up more time for the gods to relax and engage in other activities. Genesis 1 describes humanity as the pinnacle of God’s creation. Humans are not an afterthought or a nuisance. They are the only aspect of creation God describes as “very good” (Genesis 1:31).

Creation of Humankind (2:4–25)

While Genesis 1–2:3 offers a 30,000-foot view of creation, Genesis 2:4–25 presents a more detailed account of God’s creative acts, especially as it relates to the creation of humanity. Contrary to some modern accounts of humanity’s origin, which often include a version of evolution, the Genesis account teaches us that God intentionally created humans. Humans did not manage to win the evolutionary battle.

Genesis 2:4–25 demonstrates God’s creation of and attentive care for humanity. First, God fashions the man out of the ground and breathes life into him. God names the man Adam, which comes from the Hebrew word for the “ground.” Next, the story describes the ground from which Adam was created as a paradise. It describes God as planting the garden and filling it with all sorts of trees for food. From the garden flows a river that splits into four other rivers that course through lands known for producing valuable metals.

After creating the garden, God placed Adam there because the garden needed someone to tend it. In doing so, God signaled the importance of work for humanity. From the very beginning, God designed humans for a purpose.

Finally, God recognizes that Adam should not be alone. The issue is not Adam’s loneliness, but a need for someone to assist him in tending the garden (Goldingay 2020, 62). So God creates a “helper” for Adam. The method of creation for this helper differs from Adam’s creation. God does not form Eve out of the ground but from one of Adam’s ribs. The similarity of the two creatures is even seen in the words used to describe them. In Hebrew, the word for man is ish , and the word for woman is isha . By the end of the chapter, humanity exists in harmony with creation and the Creator. This harmony is symbolized by the couple’s nakedness and lack of shame.

The Fall (Genesis 3–5)

Describe the Fall and the Flood.

Two life-altering events stand out in the history of humankind: (1) humanity’s fall in the Garden and (2) Christ’s coming to save fallen humanity. Even as the Book of Genesis records the account of the Fall , it anticipates the day when Christ would come to save humanity from sin. Therefore, the Book of Genesis plays a pivotal role in establishing a Christian worldview that includes the origin of humanity, the central problem humans face, and God’s plan to redeem His creation.

Adam and Eve (3:1–24)

The Scriptures show that the fall of humankind and its results are real. Adam and Eve were real people whose relationship to God depended on trust and obedience. However, they chose to disobey because they doubted God’s Word. They listened to the tempter and sinned by obeying his suggestions. Because of this sin, God judged the serpent, Satan, Eve, and Adam (Genesis 3:1–24; Romans 5:12, 15–19).

Yet God gave Adam and Eve mercy before judgment. How? He promised them that the Seed of the woman would defeat the seed of the serpent (Genesis 3:15). This was the first promise of the coming Messiah. Later in the Old Testament, God would give more details about the promised Messiah (Genesis 12:1–3; Numbers 24:17–19; 2 Samuel 7; 1 Chronicles 17:11–14; Isaiah 7:14; 9:6–7).

After showing mercy by promising Adam and Eve a Savior, God showed judgment by driving them out of the Garden. At that moment, they began to feel the full weight of the curse that sin brought. Still, in the midst of His judgment, God displayed His love by clothing them with animal skins.

Although God punished Adam and Eve for disobeying Him, He gave them hope for the future. When Cain was born, Eve expressed that hope by saying, “With the help of the Lord I have brought forth a man” (Genesis 4:1). She knew that, through the continuation of the human line, someday God’s promised Redeemer would crush the serpent’s head (3:15). From one generation to another, others held on to the hope of victory over sin and the serpent and thus kept the promise of redemption alive.

Cain (4:1–24)

However, Eve’s hope of a Redeemer would not be realized in Cain, who became the first murderer. Cain disobeyed by offering God a sacrifice that did not please Him (Genesis 4:3–5). It is not clear whether God instructed Cain and Abel about what type of sacrifice they should bring or why God was displeased with Cain’s offering. Although the biblical account acknowledges sin in Cain’s life (4:6–7), it does not specifically state what that sin is. In any case, the events surrounding the sacrifice resulted in Cain’s anger and jealousy, which led him to sin by murdering his brother (4:8; 1 John 3:12). God punished Cain by placing him under a curse and condemning him to wander the earth.

Genesis 4:17–24 includes a summary of Cain and his family tree, which may cover a long period of time. Some of Cain’s descendants raised flocks and herds. In time, they developed many useful things, including musical instruments. They learned how to forge metal such as bronze and iron into tools and weapons. Because of their progress, the people began to feel safe. They depended on themselves, not on God.

The Bible presents Lamech, a descendant of Cain, as a very proud man. He was the first man we know of to reject God’s plan of monogamy (Genesis 4:19). Also, he bragged about killing a man who hurt him. Lamech did not believe in an eye for an eye; he believed in a life for a bruise (4:23). Lamech considered himself ten times more valuable than Cain (4:24). This short story shows the expanding problem sin posed in the world after Adam and Eve’s initial disobedience.

Seth (4:25–5:32)

Adam and Eve renewed their hope in God when Seth was born. Eve said, “God has granted me another child in place of Abel, since Cain killed him” (Genesis 4:25). Seth’s legacy turned out to be very different from Cain’s.

As we examine Seth’s lineage, it is important to understand the approach of biblical cultures to compiling genealogies. The Hebrews used the word son much like we use the term descendant today. It indicated any relationship to an ancestor. Thus, a son (descendant) in the biblical text could refer to a son, grandson, great-grandson, and so on. As a result, the biblical genealogies are selective, not comprehensive. This allows genealogies to cover long periods of time, highlighting only key descendants. The genealogy of Genesis 5 covers a great deal of history, yet moves the narrative ahead quickly (Arnold 1998, 58).

With this in mind, we note that Seth’s descendants included Enosh (his son), Kenan, Mahalalel, Jared, Enoch, Methuselah, Lamech, and Noah. Several of those in Seth’s family line were godly men. In the days of Enosh, people “began to call on the name of the Lord” (Genesis 4:26).

Enoch was born several centuries after Seth and Enosh. He was a godly person, well known for his lifestyle of close fellowship with God. In the brief account of his life, the Bible twice emphasizes that Enoch walked with God (5:22, 24). He had such a close relationship with God that his life did not end in death. Rather, God raptured Enoch, taking him up to heaven while he was still alive.

The godly example of Seth’s line continued in Noah’s father, Lamech (a different Lamech than Cain’s descendant). The Scriptures record that Lamech believed and hoped in God. When Noah was born, Lamech prophesied, “He will comfort us in the labor and painful toil of our hands caused by the ground the Lord has cursed” (5:29). Lamech believed God would provide a way for people to be set free from the curse of sin that had expelled Adam and Eve from the Garden.

The Flood (Genesis 6:1–8:17)

While we commonly think of Genesis 3 as the story of the Fall, humanity’s descent into sin continues throughout much of Genesis 1–11. When readers arrive at Genesis 6:1, they discover just how pervasive the problem of sin had become. Humanity sinned against God so much that all of their thoughts were bent toward evil. Eight references to the earth in Genesis 6:5–13 emphasize the universal problem of sin (Hamilton 2005, 273). Human sinfulness was not confined to one location.

The story of the Flood shows that God does not react erratically to sin. Instead, He is heartbroken over humanity’s unfaithfulness (Genesis 6:6). God’s act of judgment against sin is characterized as an act of de-creation. Genesis 6:7 refers to the animals in ways that recall Genesis 1. Because humanity’s sin ruined God’s good creation, God systematically tore creation down so that He could renew it.

The attentive reader already knows that Noah will play a significant role in God’s plan. When Noah first appears in the genealogy of Genesis 5, his father predicts that Noah will help lift God’s curse from humanity (Genesis 5:29). Noah obeyed the Lord by building an ark to protect himself and his family. He housed both clean and unclean animals and stayed on the boat for approximately one year (compare Genesis 7:1–13 with 8:13–19) (Williams 2003b, 128). Noah does not fulfill his father’s prophecy in the way his father might have expected, but he does find favor in God’s eyes. Ultimately, his faithfulness to the Lord leads to humanity’s survival.

Although the Flood was a divine act of judgment, God also used the Flood to renew His covenant with humankind. We learn from this account that God tempers His judgment with His mercy. As “Noah found favor in the eyes of the Lord” (Genesis 6:8), people today can also be recipients of God’s grace.

A New Beginning (Genesis 8:18–11:32)

God’s Covenant with Noah (8:20–9:17)

God expressed His grace by giving people a brand new opportunity in the post-Flood world. When Noah left the ark, he worshipped God with an animal sacrifice. In response, God promised that He would never again destroy the human race and every living creature with a flood. The sign of the rainbow sealed His promise.

However, this did not mean that sin was now totally absent. Even as Noah presented his sacrifice after coming out of the ark, God acknowledged that “every inclination of the human heart is evil from childhood” (8:21). The Flood did not erase humankind’s sin nature. The sin nature would still express itself.

Noah and His Sons (9:18–10:32)

The sin nature’s continued existence is illustrated in the story of Ham, one of Noah’s sons. Ham failed to demonstrate respect for his father when he found Noah drunk and uncovered (9:20–22). As a result, Noah cursed Canaan, Ham’s son, saying that Canaan’s sons would be servants of servants. Some people have wrongly interpreted this event, attributing the slavery of African Americans to their descent from Canaan. Such a conclusion is wholly incorrect. The Canaanites were not the same color as African Americans. More importantly, the color of a person’s skin does not matter to God. He loves everyone and shows no favoritism.

Ham was the youngest of Noah’s three sons. His older brothers were Japheth and Shem. As noted in Genesis 10, each of Noah’s sons migrated to a different area: Japheth toward Spain and northern Asia Minor, Ham toward Africa (10:6–14), and Shem near the Persian Gulf (10:21–31).

The Tower of Babel (11:1–9)

After the Flood, the human race was one group that spoke only one language. Although God had originally directed Noah’s family to “fill the earth” (Genesis 9:1), many individuals settled on the Plain of Shinar for quite some time (11:1–2). As their pride and arrogance in their abilities and skills grew, they purposed to build a tower at Babel—a great tower that would reach to the heavens. The location of the tower is ironic because it would later develop into Babylon. In this way, the tower not only represents humanity’s temporary disobedience to God’s command, but it also symbolizes a destructive force throughout the history of God’s people. The Babylonians conquer Jerusalem in 587 and Babylon symbolizes opposition to God in the Book of Revelation.

Humanity’s main motivation was “so that we may make a name for ourselves” (11:4). However, God confused their language to hinder communication and cooperation and to halt their plans. As a result, people finally migrated to several different locations according to God’s original instructions.

The Messianic Line of Shem (11:10–32)

Genesis 11 ends with a synopsis of Shem’s lineage. The children of Shem are called “Shemites” (Semites), from which the word Semitic is derived. The list in Genesis 11 names ten families and ends with Terah, who moved from Ur to Haran. Terah was Abraham’s father. Through this nation, the promise of a Messiah would be fulfilled (Genesis 22:15–18; Matthew 1:1–2). Genesis 12–50 discusses the early patriarchs, whom we will study in the next chapter.

**Q:** Why is Moses considered to have written the Pentateuch?

**A:** In general, the Bible and all of Jewish and Christian tradition credit Moses as the author of the Pentateuch.

**Q:** Explain three views about creation.

**Q:** Give an example of God’s judgment and of His mercy in the Garden.

**A:** God punished Adam and Eve for disobeying Him by driving them out of the Garden. He showed them mercy by promising a Savior, the hope for the future.

**Q:** Compare Cain’s family record with Seth’s descendants.

**A:** God is not mentioned in Cain’s family tree; the people depended on themselves, not on God. Seth’s lineage included notable, godly men.

**Q:** Why are the Fall and the Flood significant Old Testament events?

**A:** Both events show that sin and evil existed, and because it was constantly increasing, God was sorry that He had created people. In both events, God’s judgment was tempered by His mercy and grace. 2 CHAPTER

#### 1.2.3 — Describe the Fall and the Flood.

The first Hebrew word in the Book of Genesis means “in the beginning.” The English title, “Genesis,” comes from the Greek translation of this word and means “origin.” Thus, the Book of Genesis is about the origin of creation. Its first eleven chapters describe Creation, the Fall, and the Flood. While we may want more information about these events, God inspired this section of Scripture to emphasize what He deems most important.

The stories recorded in these opening chapters of the Bible prove to be foundational for everything that comes after. In Genesis 1–11, we encounter the problem of sin and God’s plan to address it through the Redeemer. By the time we arrive at Genesis 12, we are ready to see how God establishes His covenant with the patriarchs in Genesis 12–50.

Since this is a survey course, we cannot cover every section of the Old Testament as thoroughly as we might like. Even so, the stories in Genesis are so pivotal for the biblical story that we will devote the first two chapters to the study of Genesis.

Discuss authorship issues regarding the Pentateuch.

Authorship

As noted in the previous lesson, the first five books of the Bible are called the Torah or the Pentateuch. The Hebrew word torah means law or instruction. In Greek, pente means “five,” and teuchos means “scroll” or “book.” Thus, Pentateuch means “five books.” It includes Genesis, Exodus, Leviticus, Numbers, and Deuteronomy.

Who wrote the first five books of the Bible? This question raises serious issues and can be a challenging topic for some people to consider. At the most foundational level, God is the author. Through the Holy Spirit, He inspired the composition of Genesis through Deuteronomy (and the rest of the Bible). But what human did God’s Spirit inspire to write these books? Strictly speaking, the author of Genesis through Deuteronomy never identifies himself. In this sense, the books are anonymous. Even so, Jewish and Christian traditions have historically credited Moses as the author of these books.

Not everyone agrees that Moses wrote all of the Pentateuch, however. People who adopt this position point to several pieces of information. First, the final section of Deuteronomy references Moses’s death and is written from a later perspective (Deuteronomy 34:1–12). Second, some references appear to require a later date. For example, Genesis 21:32 references the Philistines, but the Philistines did not come to the land of Israel until much later in history. Third, many of the stories in Genesis originated centuries before Moses’s time. For these reasons and more, some people reject Mosaic authorship completely.

Many people, however, still believe that Moses wrote the Pentateuch. Their arguments rely, in part, on the weight of tradition. For the vast majority of history, the Jewish and Christian traditions held to Mosaic authorship. Additionally, while nothing in the Pentateuch ever attributes the whole collection to Moses, there are numerous references to Moses’s writing activities. For example, “God commands him to record certain historical events (Ex. 17:14; Num. 33:2) and laws (Ex. 24:4; 34:27) as well as a song (Deut. 31:22; see Deut. 32)” (Longman 1994, 41).

Some people prefer to combine aspects of the different arguments. For example, Moses must have used ancient sources for many of the stories in the Book of Genesis. He likely relied on oral tradition and perhaps some written material to compose the first book of the Bible. Additionally, passages like the one at the end of Deuteronomy suggest that someone or some people edited the Pentateuch in the years following Moses’s life. Even so, the internal biblical evidence strongly suggests that Moses wrote the material found in the Pentateuch.

Tremper Longman III offers a helpful concluding summary:

The conservative view has always been qualified, however subtly, by admitting non-Mosaic elements to the Torah. While in the minds of most, these non-Mosaic parts are few and far between, it does indicate that to speak of Moses as author of the Pentateuch is not the same as saying that every word is the result of his work. Since there are what appear to be obvious later additions, many conservatives speak in terms of the “essential authorship” of Moses. This expression vigorously affirms Moses as the author of the Torah, while also leaving open the possibility of later canonical additions. (Longman 1994, 42)

Outline of Genesis 1–11

Genesis 1–11 is the introduction not only of the Pentateuch but also of the entire Old Testament. The New Testament quotes Genesis 1–11 at least sixty times (Horton 1995, 218). These first eleven chapters of the Bible explain humankind’s creation, fall, judgment, and scattering. Thus, they introduce God, humans, and the problem of sin.

As Genesis 1–2 teach, God created humanity in His image. Tragically, sin separated humans from God. Thankfully, God had a plan for our redemption. In Genesis 1–11, we see this in God’s prophecy about Eve (Genesis 3:15) and His preservation of humanity through Noah’s family (Genesis 6–9). While we will explore this theme of salvation and the Messiah throughout the Old Testament, it is important to see that Genesis 1–11 introduces the background and the problem that the entire Bible discusses.

I. The Creation Account (1–2)

A. Summary of all Creation (1:1–2:4)

B. Creation of Adam and Eve (2:4–25)

II. Humanity’s Fall and Its Results (3–5)

A. Adam and Eve’s Disobedience and Judgment (3)

B. Cain and the Ungodly Lineage (4:1–24)

C. Seth and the Godly Lineage (4:25–5:32)

III. The Flood: God’s Judgment (6:1–8:17)

IV. Humankind’s New Beginning (8:18–11:32)

A. God’s Covenant with Noah (8:20–9:17)

B. Noah and His Sons (9:18–10:32)

C. The Tower of Babel (11:1–9)

D. The Messianic Line of Shem (11:10–32)

Evaluate three viewpoints about Creation.

The Creation Account (Genesis 1–2)

The first two chapters of Genesis establish critical aspects of the Christian worldview. They explain that God intentionally created the universe and everything in it. These chapters also describe the origin and purpose of human existence. They do not provide us with a detailed, scientific explanation of creation. Instead, they simply presuppose that God created everything and emphasize the theological points God intended for us to learn.

Views Concerning Creation

While Genesis 1 – 2 should not be read as scientific accounts of creation, many people still wonder how these stories relate to the origin of the universe. Over the years, scholars have offered several proposals.

The Gap Theory (1:1–31)

Supporters of the gap theory think that a time gap occurred between the first two verses of Genesis. That is, they believe Genesis 1:1 shows God’s first creation, which was afterward destroyed by God’s judgment. Verse 2 then describes a dark, fallen creation many years later. Some refer to the prophecy against “the ruler of Tyre” in Ezekiel 28, linking the dark condition in Genesis 1:2 to the fall of Satan and his angels (Luke 10:18). According to the gap theory, Genesis 1:3–31 describes how God restored creation after Satan’s fall.

The Step-by-Step View (1:1–31)

This view states that Genesis 1:1–2 describes the first step God took in creation. That is, God created the heavens and earth like a lump of clay, ready for a potter to shape. Then, in Days 1–3, God formed the earth. In Days 4–6, He filled the earth (Horton 1995, 226–227).

Step or Act of Creation

Genesis

God created the heavens and earth, dark and empty.

1:1–2

Day 1: Light

1:3–5

Day 2: Air between heavens and earth

1:6–8

Day 3: Dry ground, plants, and trees

1:9–13

Day 4: Light-bearers (sun, moon, and stars)

1:14–19

Day 5: Fish and birds

1:20–23

Day 6: Animals and humans

1:24–31

Figure 1.4

Even within the step-by-step view, people have many different beliefs about how long God took to complete creation. While the Bible says the work of creation took six days, it does not explain the length of each day. As with other Scripture passages, the word day in Genesis 1–11 may mean a long period of time. Peter tells us that with the Lord, “a day is like a thousand years, and a thousand years are like a day” (2 Peter 3:8). On the other hand, a day in Scripture often refers to twenty-four hours (Genesis 8:12).

The Literary Framework View (1:1–31)

Some Bible interpreters understand the organization of the days in Genesis 1 to be topical and theological, not necessarily chronological or sequential (Moreland and Reynolds 1999, 138). This is called the literary framework view because of the literary structure of the biblical text. These interpreters view Days 4–6 as being parallel to Days 1–3. For example, Day 4 is parallel to Day 1 in that both days deal with the subject of light. Days 2 and 5 are similar in that waters and sky are the theme; yet in Day 5, the animals that inhabit these areas are also mentioned. The third and sixth days deal with land, although the creation of animals and humankind are mentioned in the account of the sixth day (Blocher 1984, 51).

The important thing in all of these views is that God is acknowledged as our Creator and Provider.

Genesis 1 and Other Ancient Near East Accounts

While hypotheses about the relationship between the creation of the world and the details of Genesis 1 remain interesting, they should not overshadow the theological emphases God inspired in the text. When one considers the creation account within its ancient Near Eastern (ANE) context, several important points become apparent.

First, God has no rival. Other creation accounts in the ancient Near East describe a cosmic battle as the origin of the universe. Sometimes, the god who creates the world uses the deceased corpse of another defeated deity to fashion creation. The God of the Bible stands unopposed at the beginning of the Bible. He does not need to battle anyone and He creates the universe by speaking it into existence (Longman 1994, 52).

Second, God creates humanity in His image. Other ancient Near Eastern accounts describe humanity as an afterthought or nuisance to the gods. The gods create humans to have them do menial tasks and free up more time for the gods to relax and engage in other activities. Genesis 1 describes humanity as the pinnacle of God’s creation. Humans are not an afterthought or a nuisance. They are the only aspect of creation God describes as “very good” (Genesis 1:31).

Creation of Humankind (2:4–25)

While Genesis 1–2:3 offers a 30,000-foot view of creation, Genesis 2:4–25 presents a more detailed account of God’s creative acts, especially as it relates to the creation of humanity. Contrary to some modern accounts of humanity’s origin, which often include a version of evolution, the Genesis account teaches us that God intentionally created humans. Humans did not manage to win the evolutionary battle.

Genesis 2:4–25 demonstrates God’s creation of and attentive care for humanity. First, God fashions the man out of the ground and breathes life into him. God names the man Adam, which comes from the Hebrew word for the “ground.” Next, the story describes the ground from which Adam was created as a paradise. It describes God as planting the garden and filling it with all sorts of trees for food. From the garden flows a river that splits into four other rivers that course through lands known for producing valuable metals.

After creating the garden, God placed Adam there because the garden needed someone to tend it. In doing so, God signaled the importance of work for humanity. From the very beginning, God designed humans for a purpose.

Finally, God recognizes that Adam should not be alone. The issue is not Adam’s loneliness, but a need for someone to assist him in tending the garden (Goldingay 2020, 62). So God creates a “helper” for Adam. The method of creation for this helper differs from Adam’s creation. God does not form Eve out of the ground but from one of Adam’s ribs. The similarity of the two creatures is even seen in the words used to describe them. In Hebrew, the word for man is ish , and the word for woman is isha . By the end of the chapter, humanity exists in harmony with creation and the Creator. This harmony is symbolized by the couple’s nakedness and lack of shame.

The Fall (Genesis 3–5)

Describe the Fall and the Flood.

Two life-altering events stand out in the history of humankind: (1) humanity’s fall in the Garden and (2) Christ’s coming to save fallen humanity. Even as the Book of Genesis records the account of the Fall , it anticipates the day when Christ would come to save humanity from sin. Therefore, the Book of Genesis plays a pivotal role in establishing a Christian worldview that includes the origin of humanity, the central problem humans face, and God’s plan to redeem His creation.

Adam and Eve (3:1–24)

The Scriptures show that the fall of humankind and its results are real. Adam and Eve were real people whose relationship to God depended on trust and obedience. However, they chose to disobey because they doubted God’s Word. They listened to the tempter and sinned by obeying his suggestions. Because of this sin, God judged the serpent, Satan, Eve, and Adam (Genesis 3:1–24; Romans 5:12, 15–19).

Yet God gave Adam and Eve mercy before judgment. How? He promised them that the Seed of the woman would defeat the seed of the serpent (Genesis 3:15). This was the first promise of the coming Messiah. Later in the Old Testament, God would give more details about the promised Messiah (Genesis 12:1–3; Numbers 24:17–19; 2 Samuel 7; 1 Chronicles 17:11–14; Isaiah 7:14; 9:6–7).

After showing mercy by promising Adam and Eve a Savior, God showed judgment by driving them out of the Garden. At that moment, they began to feel the full weight of the curse that sin brought. Still, in the midst of His judgment, God displayed His love by clothing them with animal skins.

Although God punished Adam and Eve for disobeying Him, He gave them hope for the future. When Cain was born, Eve expressed that hope by saying, “With the help of the Lord I have brought forth a man” (Genesis 4:1). She knew that, through the continuation of the human line, someday God’s promised Redeemer would crush the serpent’s head (3:15). From one generation to another, others held on to the hope of victory over sin and the serpent and thus kept the promise of redemption alive.

Cain (4:1–24)

However, Eve’s hope of a Redeemer would not be realized in Cain, who became the first murderer. Cain disobeyed by offering God a sacrifice that did not please Him (Genesis 4:3–5). It is not clear whether God instructed Cain and Abel about what type of sacrifice they should bring or why God was displeased with Cain’s offering. Although the biblical account acknowledges sin in Cain’s life (4:6–7), it does not specifically state what that sin is. In any case, the events surrounding the sacrifice resulted in Cain’s anger and jealousy, which led him to sin by murdering his brother (4:8; 1 John 3:12). God punished Cain by placing him under a curse and condemning him to wander the earth.

Genesis 4:17–24 includes a summary of Cain and his family tree, which may cover a long period of time. Some of Cain’s descendants raised flocks and herds. In time, they developed many useful things, including musical instruments. They learned how to forge metal such as bronze and iron into tools and weapons. Because of their progress, the people began to feel safe. They depended on themselves, not on God.

The Bible presents Lamech, a descendant of Cain, as a very proud man. He was the first man we know of to reject God’s plan of monogamy (Genesis 4:19). Also, he bragged about killing a man who hurt him. Lamech did not believe in an eye for an eye; he believed in a life for a bruise (4:23). Lamech considered himself ten times more valuable than Cain (4:24). This short story shows the expanding problem sin posed in the world after Adam and Eve’s initial disobedience.

Seth (4:25–5:32)

Adam and Eve renewed their hope in God when Seth was born. Eve said, “God has granted me another child in place of Abel, since Cain killed him” (Genesis 4:25). Seth’s legacy turned out to be very different from Cain’s.

As we examine Seth’s lineage, it is important to understand the approach of biblical cultures to compiling genealogies. The Hebrews used the word son much like we use the term descendant today. It indicated any relationship to an ancestor. Thus, a son (descendant) in the biblical text could refer to a son, grandson, great-grandson, and so on. As a result, the biblical genealogies are selective, not comprehensive. This allows genealogies to cover long periods of time, highlighting only key descendants. The genealogy of Genesis 5 covers a great deal of history, yet moves the narrative ahead quickly (Arnold 1998, 58).

With this in mind, we note that Seth’s descendants included Enosh (his son), Kenan, Mahalalel, Jared, Enoch, Methuselah, Lamech, and Noah. Several of those in Seth’s family line were godly men. In the days of Enosh, people “began to call on the name of the Lord” (Genesis 4:26).

Enoch was born several centuries after Seth and Enosh. He was a godly person, well known for his lifestyle of close fellowship with God. In the brief account of his life, the Bible twice emphasizes that Enoch walked with God (5:22, 24). He had such a close relationship with God that his life did not end in death. Rather, God raptured Enoch, taking him up to heaven while he was still alive.

The godly example of Seth’s line continued in Noah’s father, Lamech (a different Lamech than Cain’s descendant). The Scriptures record that Lamech believed and hoped in God. When Noah was born, Lamech prophesied, “He will comfort us in the labor and painful toil of our hands caused by the ground the Lord has cursed” (5:29). Lamech believed God would provide a way for people to be set free from the curse of sin that had expelled Adam and Eve from the Garden.

The Flood (Genesis 6:1–8:17)

While we commonly think of Genesis 3 as the story of the Fall, humanity’s descent into sin continues throughout much of Genesis 1–11. When readers arrive at Genesis 6:1, they discover just how pervasive the problem of sin had become. Humanity sinned against God so much that all of their thoughts were bent toward evil. Eight references to the earth in Genesis 6:5–13 emphasize the universal problem of sin (Hamilton 2005, 273). Human sinfulness was not confined to one location.

The story of the Flood shows that God does not react erratically to sin. Instead, He is heartbroken over humanity’s unfaithfulness (Genesis 6:6). God’s act of judgment against sin is characterized as an act of de-creation. Genesis 6:7 refers to the animals in ways that recall Genesis 1. Because humanity’s sin ruined God’s good creation, God systematically tore creation down so that He could renew it.

The attentive reader already knows that Noah will play a significant role in God’s plan. When Noah first appears in the genealogy of Genesis 5, his father predicts that Noah will help lift God’s curse from humanity (Genesis 5:29). Noah obeyed the Lord by building an ark to protect himself and his family. He housed both clean and unclean animals and stayed on the boat for approximately one year (compare Genesis 7:1–13 with 8:13–19) (Williams 2003b, 128). Noah does not fulfill his father’s prophecy in the way his father might have expected, but he does find favor in God’s eyes. Ultimately, his faithfulness to the Lord leads to humanity’s survival.

Although the Flood was a divine act of judgment, God also used the Flood to renew His covenant with humankind. We learn from this account that God tempers His judgment with His mercy. As “Noah found favor in the eyes of the Lord” (Genesis 6:8), people today can also be recipients of God’s grace.

A New Beginning (Genesis 8:18–11:32)

God’s Covenant with Noah (8:20–9:17)

God expressed His grace by giving people a brand new opportunity in the post-Flood world. When Noah left the ark, he worshipped God with an animal sacrifice. In response, God promised that He would never again destroy the human race and every living creature with a flood. The sign of the rainbow sealed His promise.

However, this did not mean that sin was now totally absent. Even as Noah presented his sacrifice after coming out of the ark, God acknowledged that “every inclination of the human heart is evil from childhood” (8:21). The Flood did not erase humankind’s sin nature. The sin nature would still express itself.

Noah and His Sons (9:18–10:32)

The sin nature’s continued existence is illustrated in the story of Ham, one of Noah’s sons. Ham failed to demonstrate respect for his father when he found Noah drunk and uncovered (9:20–22). As a result, Noah cursed Canaan, Ham’s son, saying that Canaan’s sons would be servants of servants. Some people have wrongly interpreted this event, attributing the slavery of African Americans to their descent from Canaan. Such a conclusion is wholly incorrect. The Canaanites were not the same color as African Americans. More importantly, the color of a person’s skin does not matter to God. He loves everyone and shows no favoritism.

Ham was the youngest of Noah’s three sons. His older brothers were Japheth and Shem. As noted in Genesis 10, each of Noah’s sons migrated to a different area: Japheth toward Spain and northern Asia Minor, Ham toward Africa (10:6–14), and Shem near the Persian Gulf (10:21–31).

The Tower of Babel (11:1–9)

After the Flood, the human race was one group that spoke only one language. Although God had originally directed Noah’s family to “fill the earth” (Genesis 9:1), many individuals settled on the Plain of Shinar for quite some time (11:1–2). As their pride and arrogance in their abilities and skills grew, they purposed to build a tower at Babel—a great tower that would reach to the heavens. The location of the tower is ironic because it would later develop into Babylon. In this way, the tower not only represents humanity’s temporary disobedience to God’s command, but it also symbolizes a destructive force throughout the history of God’s people. The Babylonians conquer Jerusalem in 587 and Babylon symbolizes opposition to God in the Book of Revelation.

Humanity’s main motivation was “so that we may make a name for ourselves” (11:4). However, God confused their language to hinder communication and cooperation and to halt their plans. As a result, people finally migrated to several different locations according to God’s original instructions.

The Messianic Line of Shem (11:10–32)

Genesis 11 ends with a synopsis of Shem’s lineage. The children of Shem are called “Shemites” (Semites), from which the word Semitic is derived. The list in Genesis 11 names ten families and ends with Terah, who moved from Ur to Haran. Terah was Abraham’s father. Through this nation, the promise of a Messiah would be fulfilled (Genesis 22:15–18; Matthew 1:1–2). Genesis 12–50 discusses the early patriarchs, whom we will study in the next chapter.

**Q:** Why is Moses considered to have written the Pentateuch?

**A:** In general, the Bible and all of Jewish and Christian tradition credit Moses as the author of the Pentateuch.

**Q:** Give an example of God’s judgment and of His mercy in the Garden.

**A:** God punished Adam and Eve for disobeying Him by driving them out of the Garden. He showed them mercy by promising a Savior, the hope for the future.

**Q:** Compare Cain’s family record with Seth’s descendants.

**A:** God is not mentioned in Cain’s family tree; the people depended on themselves, not on God. Seth’s lineage included notable, godly men.

**Q:** Why are the Fall and the Flood significant Old Testament events?

**A:** Both events show that sin and evil existed, and because it was constantly increasing, God was sorry that He had created people. In both events, God’s judgment was tempered by His mercy and grace. 2 CHAPTER

## 2 - The Patriarchs (Genesis 12–50)

### Abraham (Genesis 12:1–25:11)

#### 2.1.1 — Identify the covenant God establishes with Abraham.

Identify the covenant God establishes with Abraham.

Abraham is one of the greatest people in history. Judaism, Islam, and Christianity all consider him to be the first patriarch and a man of great faith. Abraham’s journey of faith is described in Genesis 12–25. These chapters lay out the foundation of God’s covenant with the people of Israel and eventually all who follow Jesus.

24:1–67
