### Daniel: The Prophet of Divine Sovereignty

#### 13.2.1 — Explain the setting, author, date, and themes of Daniel.

Explain the setting, author, date, and themes of Daniel.

Author and Date

The Book of Daniel has faced more criticism about its authorship than many other Old Testament books. Scholars who reject Daniel as the author often suggest that it was written in the 160s BC. They present supposed historical inaccuracies, extremely specific prophecies that match the Middle Eastern politics of that time, and similarities with the apocalyptic genre from the second century BC.

While the Book of Daniel does present interpretive challenges, there are several reasons to affirm that Daniel wrote the book in the sixth century as is suggested by the book. First, the Book of Daniel is attested in both the Septuagint and Dead Sea Scrolls. Both of these collections were formed by the second century BC. If the book had only just been written, it is hard to imagine how it could have circulated and gained authoritative status so quickly. Second, supposed historical inaccuracies are not conclusive and there are plausible explanations for the issues (Hill and Walton 2009, 570).

The Book of Daniel spans from Nebuchadnezzar’s first invasion of Jerusalem (605 BC) to the third year of Cyrus in 536 BC (Daniel 10:1). Thus, most conservative scholars hold to a date of authorship sometime in the sixth century BC.

Setting

Daniel, whose name means “God is my judge,” was one of the last Old Testament prophets. Remember that Daniel was likely only a teenager when he was captured and taken to Babylon in 605 BC. He apparently lived through the end of the seventy years of captivity of which Jeremiah prophesied (Jeremiah 25:11–12; 29:10–14; Daniel 9:1–3).

Daniel was exiled by the Babylonians in 605 BC for governmental service. He, along with other young men from various conquered lands, was taken to the great city of Babylon. There they received an education in the Babylonian language and literature. Daniel’s divinely anointed and natural abilities propelled him into a position as a counselor to King Nebuchadnezzar. Daniel faithfully and competently served his foreign masters for many decades. In 539 BC the Persians, under Cyrus the Great, took control of Babylon. Thus Daniel’s last years were lived as an official in the Persian government. (Brubaker 2003b, 792)

Daniel 7–12 is autobiographical, relating the prophet’s own experiences, dreams, and visions. It is likely that Daniel was quite elderly when he experienced these visions.

Apocalyptic Literature

Like Revelation in the New Testament, the Book of Daniel is classified as apocalyptic literature. The term apocalyptic stems from the Greek word apokalupto , meaning “to unveil or reveal.” A common literary genre in the ancient Near East, apocalyptic literature used visions and symbols to unveil or reveal hidden things, which often included future events. In other words, apocalyptic books incorporated several common characteristics, including visions, a concern about the future, descriptions of an eschatological judgment, and a division of history into distinct chronological periods culminating in the final judgment (Arnold and Beyer 1999, 428).

Purpose and Themes

Daniel wrote his book to encourage his people that their captivity would end and that the future would be brighter. Unlike many prophets, Daniel did not tell the Jews to repent; rather, he emphasized being faithful to God in a foreign land. His actions, and those of his three friends, showed what it meant to be faithful.

The main theme of Daniel is divine sovereignty—that is, God’s rule or reign over the human kingdoms and governments established in this world. This theme of God’s sovereignty over all nations and rulers (4:17; 5:21) plays out in both political and spiritual arenas. Daniel 1–6 deals primarily with God’s sovereignty over the kingdoms in existence during the Exile and in which Daniel played a role because of divine appointment. Daniel 7–12 deals primarily with God’s sovereignty over the kingdoms that would rise and fall after Daniel’s time. Some of these kingdoms are apparently still in the future, that is, in the coming eschatological age.

The primary and secondary theological themes emerge from the narrative or story-like nature of the Book of Daniel. Along with God’s sovereignty, the book emphasizes (1) the arrogance of rulers and others who fail to acknowledge that God is indeed the Supreme Ruler and (2) the guarantee of future divine victory over all evil. Secondary themes include prayer and the general prophetic message (Arnold and Beyer 1999, 431–432).

Outline

Describe the dreams or visions in Daniel 2, 7, 8, 9, and 10–12.

The stories in Daniel 1–6 “are accounts of Daniel and his friends. They detail their education and work as administrators and wise men. These narratives resemble the centuries-old hero stories and court tales of Joseph and Moses in the land of Egypt” (Brubaker 2003b, 793). Then, in chapter 7, Daniel transitions into recounting his dreams and visions about the future events of Israel and other nations. The entire book can be outlined as follows:

I. Historical Setting (1)

A. Exiled to Babylon (1:1–7)

B. Faithfulness to God (1:8–16)

C. Promotion for Daniel and His Friends (1:17–21)

II. Daniel’s Messages About the Nations (2–7)

A. Nebuchadnezzar’s Dream of a Huge Statue (2)

B. The Gold Image and the Fiery Furnace (3)

C. Nebuchadnezzar’s Dream of the Great, Tall Tree (4)

D. Belshazzar’s Feast and Babylon’s Fall (5)

E. Darius’s Decree and Daniel’s Deliverance from Lions (6)

F. Daniel’s Dream of Four Beasts (7)

G. The Kingdom of Heaven: the Eternal Kingdom (7:13–14, 27)

III. Daniel’s Messages About Israel (8–12)

A. Daniel’s Vision of a Ram, a Goat, and a Small Horn (8)

B. Daniel’s Prayer and Vision of Seventy Sevens (9)

C. Daniel’s Vision of Israel’s Future (10–12)

Content According to Themes

Sovereignty of God

God’s sovereignty is displayed first in Daniel’s appointment to an influential position of leadership in an evil land and government. Unlike other prophetic books that emphasize divine sovereignty by pronouncing judgment on neighboring nations, Daniel shows that God uses even pagan rulers to accomplish His divine purposes. That is, God is sovereign over both nations and individuals. In the last section of his book, Daniel asserts God’s ultimate victory and sovereignty over future evil empires that detest God and the people who follow Him.

Arrogance of Leaders

Within Daniel 1–6 is a powerful rebuke of egotism, arrogance, and pride as epitomized by Babylonian kings Nebuchadnezzar (chapter 4) and Belshazzar (chapter 5). In these six chapters, God vindicates Daniel and his friends in spite of various conspiracies by other political leaders.

In Daniel’s visions in chapters 7–12, a succession of evil rulers and governments that display arrogance are eventually replaced by the kingdom of the Son of God and the Son of Man, Jesus Christ, the only One worthy of such recognition and devotion.

While we may find it easy to condemn the pride and arrogance of governmental rulers exemplified in the Book of Daniel, the message for us is just as profound: We must guard against all pride, including pride in our own spirituality. For “God opposes the proud but gives grace to the humble” (James 4:6).

Prophecies of the Future

The last six chapters of Daniel raise the question of how God will fulfill the promise of His ultimate victory and final reign. Because Daniel’s visions of eschatological events are sometimes difficult to interpret, not all agree on the possible timelines or facts of their fulfillment.

Which kingdoms?

Conservative scholars agree that the four beasts of Daniel 7 match the four kingdoms of Daniel 2. However, they disagree about which kingdoms—past, present, or future—the visions represent. Three views have been proposed:

1. A rather common evangelical position is “that the four metals in the statue predicted the four major empires of Babylon, Medo-Persia, Greece, and Rome” (Brubaker 2003b, 794).

2. A non-evangelical position identifies the kingdoms as Babylonia, Media, Persia, and Greece.

3. Some evangelicals contend that the fourth kingdom is Greece but still hold to a conservative date of authorship in the sixth century BC (Arnold and Beyer 1999, 434).

The authors of this course agree with the first viewpoint. That is, we believe that the kingdoms symbolized are Babylon, Media-Persia, Greece, and Rome:

Babylon was represented by a head of gold and the lion with wings.Soaring over all kingdoms, Babylon was as fierce as a lion, cruel, and quick to conquer and punish. The wings torn off the lion may represent the great change in Nebuchadnezzar (Daniel 4). Some individuals believe that Nebuchadnezzar was truly converted (7:4).

Media-Persia was symbolized by the statue’s chest of silver and the bear, which was raised up on one side to demonstrate the superior strength of Persia over Media.

Greece was represented by the bronze belly of the statue and the leopard with four wings and four heads. The wings demonstrated the swiftness of Greece’s conquering power, and the four heads probably represented the four generals under Alexander the Great . After Alexander’s death, his four generals divided his kingdom into four parts.

Rome was symbolized by the iron/clay in the statue and the terrible beast of Daniel 7. Note that Rome was too terrible to compare with any animal. Some believe the ten horns that came up on the beast refer to the same ten kings John mentions in Revelation 17:12. The horn with eyes and a mouth represents the Antichrist, who conquers the ten kings and rules the world (Daniel 7:8, 24). It appears that the Antichrist will persecute the Tribulation saints for three and one-half years (7:21–25; 12:7; Revelation 12:6, 14). Then Christ will defeat the kingdom of the Antichrist, and He will reign and rule forever.

What time frame?

A related issue is how to interpret phrases such as seventy “sevens” (or seventy weeks ; 9:24) and the abomination that causes desolation (11:31; 12:11). The term seventy “sevens” or seventy weeks has been variously interpreted even among conservative evangelical scholarship. Dyer and Merrill contend that “a time was parallel to a year; and a ‘time’ (1), ‘times’ (2), and ‘half a time’ (1/2) were equivalent to three and a half years, or the middle of the ‘seven’” (Dyer and Merrill 2001, 717). The numerous viewpoints on these matters are best studied in a course on eschatology. For the purposes of this course, it is enough to note that some believe the abomination that causes desolation took place in the past, around 167 or 166 BC, but many conservative interpreters see this event as yet future.

Evangelicals hold several different viewpoints about the end time, particularly about the order of events such as the Rapture , the Tribulation , and the Millennium. Three views exist concerning the Millennium (the one-thousand-year reign of Christ):

1. Premillennial: Christ will return before the Millennium begins.

2. Postmillennial: The Millennium began with Christ’s resurrection and will end with His second coming. Thus, Christ will return after the Millennium.

3. Amillennial: There is no literal Millennium because Revelation 20 is symbolic, not literal.

Within the broader category of premillennialism are three perspectives about the order of the Rapture and the Tribulation:

1. Pre-Tribulation: The rapture of the church will occur prior to the seven years of the Tribulation.

2. Mid-Tribulation: The Rapture will occur approximately halfway through the Tribulation.

3. Post-Tribulation: The Rapture will occur after the Tribulation.

Although all of these views are held by different evangelicals and Pentecostals, the Assemblies of God holds to a premillennial, pre-Tribulation viewpoint.

Unfortunately, some popular teachers have gone way beyond the clear teaching of Scripture on eschatology and have engaged in unfounded speculation about how, where, and when such prophecies will be fulfilled. History has proven many of these speculations totally false. With that in mind, ministers must remember to discern and preach the truth of the Word as it is stated. They must be clear where Scripture is clear and silent where Scripture is silent.

Still, despite misinterpretation, pervasive evil, human arrogance, and intense persecution of believers, the truth remains that God’s rule is coming! Jesus Christ is already the governor and ruler over all; yet an age is coming when He will finalize His victory and turn the spiritual reality of His kingdom into a governmental reality. Until that day, believers can rest assured that God’s sovereignty prevails, even in the difficult circumstances of life.

**Q:** Describe the literary genre in which Daniel falls.

**A:** Daniel is considered apocalyptic literature, meaning it unveils hidden things (often future events) through symbols and visions.

**Q:** What is the primary theme of Daniel, and what does it mean?

**A:** Daniel’s primary theme is divine sovereignty, or God’s supreme rule over all human kingdoms and governments.

**Q:** What are the four beasts in Daniel, and what four kingdoms do they represent?

**A:** The lion with wings symbolizes Babylon. The bear represents Media-Persia. The leopard with four wings and four heads represents Greece. The terrible beast symbolizes Rome.

**Q:** Describe the three views different evangelicals hold concerning the Millennium.

**A:** (1) Premillennial view: Christ will return before the Millennium. (2) Postmillennial view: Christ will return after the Millennium. (3) Amillennial view: A literal millennium will not occur because Revelation 20 is symbolic. 14 CHAPTER

## 14 - The Postexilic Prophets (Haggai, Zechariah, Malachi)

### Haggai: The Prophet of Rebuilding the Temple

#### 14.1.1 — Summarize the setting, purposes, and theme of Haggai.

Summarize the setting, purposes, and theme of Haggai.

Author, Date, and Setting

The prophet Haggai, the author of Haggai, is mentioned nine times in this book and twice in Ezra (Ezra 5:1; 6:14). We know little about Haggai except that he was an exile who responded to Cyrus’s invitation to return to the homeland. We also know that his prophetic ministry began in 520 BC, likely during the fall season (Arnold and Beyer 1999, 464).

According to Ezra 3:8–10, the first group of Jews laid the foundation for the new temple in 536 BC. However, soon after this, opposition arose among the Samaritans and other people, and work on the temple ceased in 534 BC. Discouraged, the people stopped working on God’s house and started building their own homes. No work on the temple occurred for sixteen years! Then Haggai and Zechariah began to encourage and exhort the people to begin rebuilding the temple again. Four years later, the people finished the temple, and it was dedicated in 517–516 BC (Ezra 4–6).

Purposes and Theme

Soon after returning from exile, the Jews had turned to selfish priorities because of persistent opposition and discouragement. They had allowed concern for their own well-being to replace concern for spiritual matters. Thus, Haggai’s message was intended to set their priorities straight. In other words, his primary purpose and theme was to inspire the Jews to rebuild the temple and therefore receive God’s blessings. Specifically, Haggai prophesied to encourage Zerubbabel, Judah’s governor, and Joshua, the high priest, to lead the Jews in the work of rebuilding the temple.

Outline

Discuss how Haggai conveyed his theme.

Haggai’s theme is conveyed through four messages, as outlined below:

I. First Message: Put God First—Finish the Temple (1)

A. Date: 1 Elul (August 29) 520 BC (1:1)

B. Haggai Rebukes the People (1:2–11)

C. The People’s Response (1:12–15)

II. Second Message: God’s Presence Matters More Than Beauty (2:1–9)

A. Date: 21 Tishri (October 17) 520 BC (2:1)

B. Zerubbabel’s Temple Compared to Solomon’s Temple (2:2–9)

III. Third Message: It Is Easier to Spread Sin Than Holiness (2:10–19)

A. Date: 24 Kislev (December 18) 520 BC (2:10)

B. Sin Corrupts (2:11–14)

C. Obedience Brings Blessings (2:15–19)

IV. Fourth Message: The Day of the Lord Will Exalt the Godly (2:20–23)

A. Date: 24 Kislev (December 18) 520 BC (2:20)

B. God Will Shake and Judge the Nations (2:21–22)

C. Zerubbabel Will Be Like a Signet Ring (2:23)

First Message (Haggai 1)

The first message was an exhortation to get to work (1:1–15). Haggai asserted that the people had become more concerned with building their own places of residency than with rebuilding the temple. Note that Haggai was not condemning legitimate concern for building one’s own place of lodging; rather, he was rebuking the people for focusing on selfish matters more than spiritual issues.

Because of the Jews’ misplaced priorities, their crops were scarce and they ran out of money too quickly (1:5–6). These issues were signs of covenant violation according to the Mosaic law. Yet they lived in exotic homes they had built themselves, all the while ignoring the need to rebuild the temple where they were to worship God. Haggai’s rebuke succeeded, and the people went into action. Within one month, work on the temple had begun again (Arnold and Beyer 1999, 464).

Second Message (Haggai 2:1–9)

The second message assured the people of God’s presence. It encouraged them that the Spirit of the living God was with them, assisting them in their efforts (2:1–9). Even though the new temple was not as glorious as Solomon’s temple, the people could trust that God’s glory would somehow be even greater in this temple. Haggai’s message encouraged the people to continue despite outward appearances.

Third Message (Haggai 2:10–19)

The third message strongly warned the people against becoming spiritually contaminated through close association with ungodly practices and people (2:10–19). Holiness or purity cannot be passed from one thing or person to another; it must come from an individual’s heart. However, as disease and uncleanness spread through touching, impurity and ungodliness are highly contagious through close association. Thus, the people’s return to consistent sinful practices had defiled everything they touched. Note that God does not forbid us to develop friendships with unbelievers; He simply warns us to guard against ungodly influences. The Lord then ended Haggai’s third message with a promise of blessing based on His people’s obedience.

Fourth Message (Haggai 2:20–23)

The fourth message declared that God had appointed Zerubbabel as leader and that the Day of the Lord would exalt the godly. As in the Book of Daniel, Haggai affirmed that God is the one who exalts nations and rulers and removes them from political power.

Overall, the fourth message concerned the restoration of the Davidic kingdom. It is a messianic message in that Zerubbabel is a descendant of David, he is called “God’s signet ring” as a sign of authority, and he is specifically mentioned in the genealogy of Jesus in Matthew 1:12 (Brubaker 2003a, 809).

**Q:** What was Haggai’s primary theme?

**A:** He encouraged the Jews—specifically Zeruabbabel and Joshua—to finish rebuilding the temple. Then they would receive God’s blessings.

**Q:** What four messages convey Haggai’s theme?

**A:** (1) Put God first by finishing the temple (1:1–15). (2) God’s presence matters more than beauty (2:1–9). (3) It is easier to spread sin than holiness (2:10–19). (4) The Day of the Lord will exalt the godly.

**Q:** Which message in Haggai contained strong warnings, and what were the warnings against?

**A:** The third message warned against being spiritually contaminated by close association with ungodly practices and people. The people’s return to consistent sinful practices defiled everything they touched.

**Q:** Why is the fourth message considered messianic?

**A:** Zerubbabel is a descendant of David, he is called “God’s signet ring” as a sign of authority, and he is specifically mentioned in the genealogy of Jesus in Matthew 1:12.

#### 14.1.2 — Discuss how Haggai conveyed his theme.

Summarize the setting, purposes, and theme of Haggai.

Author, Date, and Setting

The prophet Haggai, the author of Haggai, is mentioned nine times in this book and twice in Ezra (Ezra 5:1; 6:14). We know little about Haggai except that he was an exile who responded to Cyrus’s invitation to return to the homeland. We also know that his prophetic ministry began in 520 BC, likely during the fall season (Arnold and Beyer 1999, 464).

According to Ezra 3:8–10, the first group of Jews laid the foundation for the new temple in 536 BC. However, soon after this, opposition arose among the Samaritans and other people, and work on the temple ceased in 534 BC. Discouraged, the people stopped working on God’s house and started building their own homes. No work on the temple occurred for sixteen years! Then Haggai and Zechariah began to encourage and exhort the people to begin rebuilding the temple again. Four years later, the people finished the temple, and it was dedicated in 517–516 BC (Ezra 4–6).

Purposes and Theme

Soon after returning from exile, the Jews had turned to selfish priorities because of persistent opposition and discouragement. They had allowed concern for their own well-being to replace concern for spiritual matters. Thus, Haggai’s message was intended to set their priorities straight. In other words, his primary purpose and theme was to inspire the Jews to rebuild the temple and therefore receive God’s blessings. Specifically, Haggai prophesied to encourage Zerubbabel, Judah’s governor, and Joshua, the high priest, to lead the Jews in the work of rebuilding the temple.

Outline

Discuss how Haggai conveyed his theme.

Haggai’s theme is conveyed through four messages, as outlined below:

I. First Message: Put God First—Finish the Temple (1)

A. Date: 1 Elul (August 29) 520 BC (1:1)

B. Haggai Rebukes the People (1:2–11)

C. The People’s Response (1:12–15)

II. Second Message: God’s Presence Matters More Than Beauty (2:1–9)

A. Date: 21 Tishri (October 17) 520 BC (2:1)

B. Zerubbabel’s Temple Compared to Solomon’s Temple (2:2–9)

III. Third Message: It Is Easier to Spread Sin Than Holiness (2:10–19)

A. Date: 24 Kislev (December 18) 520 BC (2:10)

B. Sin Corrupts (2:11–14)

C. Obedience Brings Blessings (2:15–19)

IV. Fourth Message: The Day of the Lord Will Exalt the Godly (2:20–23)

A. Date: 24 Kislev (December 18) 520 BC (2:20)

B. God Will Shake and Judge the Nations (2:21–22)

C. Zerubbabel Will Be Like a Signet Ring (2:23)

First Message (Haggai 1)

The first message was an exhortation to get to work (1:1–15). Haggai asserted that the people had become more concerned with building their own places of residency than with rebuilding the temple. Note that Haggai was not condemning legitimate concern for building one’s own place of lodging; rather, he was rebuking the people for focusing on selfish matters more than spiritual issues.

Because of the Jews’ misplaced priorities, their crops were scarce and they ran out of money too quickly (1:5–6). These issues were signs of covenant violation according to the Mosaic law. Yet they lived in exotic homes they had built themselves, all the while ignoring the need to rebuild the temple where they were to worship God. Haggai’s rebuke succeeded, and the people went into action. Within one month, work on the temple had begun again (Arnold and Beyer 1999, 464).

Second Message (Haggai 2:1–9)

The second message assured the people of God’s presence. It encouraged them that the Spirit of the living God was with them, assisting them in their efforts (2:1–9). Even though the new temple was not as glorious as Solomon’s temple, the people could trust that God’s glory would somehow be even greater in this temple. Haggai’s message encouraged the people to continue despite outward appearances.

Third Message (Haggai 2:10–19)

The third message strongly warned the people against becoming spiritually contaminated through close association with ungodly practices and people (2:10–19). Holiness or purity cannot be passed from one thing or person to another; it must come from an individual’s heart. However, as disease and uncleanness spread through touching, impurity and ungodliness are highly contagious through close association. Thus, the people’s return to consistent sinful practices had defiled everything they touched. Note that God does not forbid us to develop friendships with unbelievers; He simply warns us to guard against ungodly influences. The Lord then ended Haggai’s third message with a promise of blessing based on His people’s obedience.

Fourth Message (Haggai 2:20–23)

The fourth message declared that God had appointed Zerubbabel as leader and that the Day of the Lord would exalt the godly. As in the Book of Daniel, Haggai affirmed that God is the one who exalts nations and rulers and removes them from political power.

Overall, the fourth message concerned the restoration of the Davidic kingdom. It is a messianic message in that Zerubbabel is a descendant of David, he is called “God’s signet ring” as a sign of authority, and he is specifically mentioned in the genealogy of Jesus in Matthew 1:12 (Brubaker 2003a, 809).

**Q:** What was Haggai’s primary theme?

**A:** He encouraged the Jews—specifically Zeruabbabel and Joshua—to finish rebuilding the temple. Then they would receive God’s blessings.

**Q:** What four messages convey Haggai’s theme?

**A:** (1) Put God first by finishing the temple (1:1–15). (2) God’s presence matters more than beauty (2:1–9). (3) It is easier to spread sin than holiness (2:10–19). (4) The Day of the Lord will exalt the godly.

**Q:** Which message in Haggai contained strong warnings, and what were the warnings against?

**A:** The third message warned against being spiritually contaminated by close association with ungodly practices and people. The people’s return to consistent sinful practices defiled everything they touched.

**Q:** Why is the fourth message considered messianic?

**A:** Zerubbabel is a descendant of David, he is called “God’s signet ring” as a sign of authority, and he is specifically mentioned in the genealogy of Jesus in Matthew 1:12.

### Zechariah: The Prophet of Visionary Encouragement

#### 14.2.1 — Describe the times, purposes, and eight visions of Zechariah.

Describe the times, purposes, and eight visions of Zechariah.

Author, Date, and Setting

The Book of Zechariah can be divided into two major sections: chapters 1–8 and chapters 9–14. Although scholars agree that the prophet Zechariah wrote the first section around 520–518 BC, they disagree about authorship of the second section. Some speculate that a second or even a third author wrote Zechariah 9–14. They point to differences in literary styles and vocabulary as well as the changing historical and political circumstances to support their position. However, conservative scholars affirm the unity of the book and attribute authorship of all chapters to Zechariah. They emphasize similarities in vocabulary and literary style in the two sections and assert that any differences do not necessarily demonstrate a change of authorship.

Therefore, this course considers the author of the entire book to be Zechariah (1:1). While the Old Testament lists some thirty-two different individuals with the same name, this was Zechariah, son of Berekiah. Like Jeremiah and Ezekiel, he was both a priest and a prophet. Zechariah was a Levite and the head of the priestly family of Iddo (1:1; Nehemiah 12:16). He was born in Babylon and apparently returned to Jerusalem with the first group of exiles.

Purposes and Themes

Like Haggai, Zechariah encouraged the rebuilding of the temple. This was his theme and purpose in chapters 1–8, as seen in the eight visions God gave him. Some scholars note that these chapters are best described as “historical eschatology” (Brubaker 2003a, 810).

Then, in chapters 9–14, Zechariah’s theme could be called the rebuilding of the nation or, more accurately, the kingdom of God. This section includes two prophecies about the Messiah to respond to the Jews’ discouragement that the Messiah had not yet come. One messianic prophecy related to Jesus’ first coming as described in the New Testament. However, the other prophecy is yet to be fulfilled. It emphasizes the eschatological age to come when Christ will establish His final, eternal kingdom.

Outline

I. Eight Visions (1:7–6:8)

A. The Horseman Among the Myrtle Trees (1:7–17)

B. The Four Horns and Four Craftsmen (1:18–21)

C. A Man Measuring Jerusalem (2)

D. The Cleansing of Joshua the High Priest (3)

E. The Gold Lampstand and Two Olive Trees (4)

F. The Flying Scroll (5:1–4)

G. The Woman in a Basket (5:5–11)

H. The Four Chariots (6:1–8)

II. The Symbolic Crowning of Joshua the High Priest (6:9–15)

III. Fasting and the Future (7–8)

A. Fasting and Social Justice (7)

B. Zion Will Be Restored (8)

IV. Two Prophecies on Israel and the Messiah (9–14)

A. The Messiah Will Come as a Shepherd King but Be Rejected (9–11)

B. The Messiah Will Come as a King and Be Accepted (12–14)

The Eight Visions (Zechariah 1:7–6:8)

The early chapters of Zechariah note past disobedience and resulting judgments, the forgiveness and cleansing of sin through the coming of the Messiah, and Spirit empowerment to complete the rebuilding of the temple. The eight visions described occur at night and in Jerusalem.

Eight Visions in Zechariah (Stamps 1992, 1372–1376)

Vision

Explanation

1. Horseman among the myrtle trees (1:7–17)

While other nations were secure and at rest, Judah suffered seventy years and afterward from famine, mildew, and hail (Haggai 1:9–11; 2:15–17). God would judge other nations and restore Judah.

2. Four horns and four craftsmen (1:18–21)

The four horns probably represent Egypt, Assyria, Babylonia, and Media-Persia, all of whom scattered Judah, Israel, and Jerusalem. The four craftsmen represent those who judged and punished the four horns.

3. A man measuring Jerusalem (2)

God would fully restore Judah. He would live among the Jews, and many other nations would also become His people.

4. Cleansing of Joshua the high priest (3)

God cleansed Joshua as a symbol that He would in one day remove the sin of Judah. The stone represents Christ, who in one day brought peace and removed sin for all who accept Him.

5. Gold lampstand and two olive trees (4)

The two olive trees symbolized constant fuel (olive oil) for the lamps. The oil flowed into a bowl that had seven small pipes and then flowed down the pipes into seven lamps. In the same way, the Holy Spirit flowed through Zerubbabel and Joshua to empower others. God wanted the Jews to be a light to the nations.

6. Flying scroll (5:1–4)

Sinners would be punished and removed from Judah. God must judge sinners wherever He finds them.

7. Woman in a basket (5:5–11)

Israel’s sin and wickedness would be sent away. A sinful land like Babylonia was a fitting place to send evil (Revelation 17–18). The revival under Ezra and Nehemiah cleansed Israel of much sin.

8. Four chariots (6:1–8)

God sent His angels of war and judgment against enemy nations. Red represents war; black is for famine and death; white is for conquering; and dappled stands for pestilence. Judgment on Babylonia caused God to rest.

Note that in the vision of the high priest’s cleansing (Zechariah 3), Satan accused the high priest of sin. This represents the failure of the priesthood in Israel to serve God faithfully in their God-given capacity. Yet God rebuked Satan. God would restore the priests’ proper office and ministry in the temple. Further, He would raise up a “Branch” (3:8; 6:12) to usher in a new age of new people with a new heart. While the term Branch occurs a few times in the Old Testament, it is clear from Zechariah’s prophecy that it refers to the Messiah, Jesus Christ (Arnold and Beyer 1999, 466).

The Messianic Prophecies (Zechariah 9–14)

Summarize two prophecies about the Messiah in Zechariah 9–14.

In Zechariah 10–11, God’s anger burned against the shepherds of the day for failing to lead properly, both politically and spiritually. These leaders deserted the flock and became more concerned with their own comfort than with the care of the sheep. Like similar passages throughout the Old Testament, the vivid imagery and principles in this passage exhort ministers to give proper care to the sheep God has entrusted to them in their congregations. Ministers must keep the best interests of the sheep in the forefront of their minds and actions.

At the same time, in Zechariah 9–14, God promises the coming of the Chief Shepherd—the Messiah. Some prophecies in these chapters were completely fulfilled through Jesus’ first coming. Others were partially fulfilled when Jesus came the first time and will be completely fulfilled when He comes again. For instance, Zechariah prophesied that Israel’s king would come riding on a donkey and would bring lasting peace (9:9–17). This, of course, was partially fulfilled when Jesus rode a donkey into Jerusalem, often referred to as His triumphal entry (Matthew 21:5). But the complete fulfillment of lasting peace is obviously still to come in the eschatological age (Arnold and Beyer 1999, 468). Zechariah then prophesied that the Chief Shepherd would be struck down (13:7–9). This was completely fulfilled in the crucifixion of Jesus Christ when He died for our sins. Other New Testament fulfillments of Zechariah’s prophecies are listed in the following chart.

New Testament Fulfillments of Zechariah’s Prophecies

Zechariah

New Testament

Prophecy Fulfilled

9:9

Matthew 21:5

Jesus, a humble King, rides a donkey and brings salvation.

11:4–14

John 10:14

Jesus says that He is the Good Shepherd.

11:12

Matthew 26:15

Christ is sold for thirty pieces of silver.

12:10

John 19:37

Jesus is pierced on the cross.

14:6–7

Revelation 22:5

There will no longer be any night.

14:8

Revelation 22:1–2

A river runs out of Jerusalem.

Hill and Walton note,

Zechariah has more to say about the messianic shepherd-king than any other Old Testament book except Isaiah. This foreshadowing of the Son of Man as Messiah is explained to the disciples by Jesus himself as a method of Old Testament interpretation (cf. Luke 24:44). Our approach identifies as “messianic” only those Old Testament passages that New Testament writers explicitly cited in reference or application to the life and ministry of Jesus of Nazareth as the Christ (while recognizing that some of these citations are at best only implicitly messianic in the context of Zechariah). (Hill and Walton 2023, 492)

**Q:** How many sections is the Book of Zechariah divided into, and what is the theme of each?

**Q:** What does the vision in Zechariah 3 point out, and what does this represent?

**A:** It indicates that Satan accuses the high priest of sin, representing the failure of the priesthood in Israel to serve God faithfully in their God-given capacity.

**Q:** What are the New Testament fulfillments of Zechariah’s messianic prophecies?

**A:** Matthew 21:5: Jesus, a humble King, rides a donkey and brings salvation. John 10:14: Jesus says that He is the Good Shepherd. Matthew 26:15: Christ is sold for thirty pieces of silver. John 19:37: Jesus is pierced on the Cross. Revelation 22:5: There will no longer be any night. Revelation 22:1–2: A river runs out of Jerusalem.

### Malachi: The Prophet of Giving One’s Best

#### 14.3.1 — Discuss the setting and time of Malachi’s ministry.

Discuss the setting and time of Malachi’s ministry.

Author and Date

Because the word Malachi means “my messenger,” some scholars assert that this is not a proper name and that the authorship of Malachi is anonymous. Hill and Walton note, “Malachi is similar to other Old Testament names ending in i, such as Beeri (Gen. 26:34) and Zichri (1 Chron. 8:19). Moreover, both Jonah and Habakkuk are solitary names among the prophets. So this single occurrence of the name Malachi should not count as evidence against its use as a proper name” (Hill and Walton 2009, 702).

Malachi keeps the focus on God’s message and away from the messenger (himself). Therefore, we do not know much about this prophet other than that he lived in Jerusalem. While the book itself does not refer to particular historical dates or events, the internal biblical evidence points strongly to a date after the Exile. This evidence includes references to a governor, a temple, and similar social conditions to the age of Ezra and Nehemiah. In fact, some references and similarities indicate that Malachi may have been a contemporary of Nehemiah. However, some Bible scholars suggest that because Ezra and Nehemiah are not specifically mentioned in the book, Malachi’s prophetic ministry may have occurred before they came to Judah (Brubaker 2003a, 833). Due to this uncertainty, we cannot pin the date of Malachi’s writing to a specific date, but we can surmise that Malachi wrote his book sometime in the range of 470–420 BC.

Setting

As with Haggai and Zechariah, the historical background of Malachi involves the political, social, and religious conditions that existed after the Exile. Having returned and settled into their homeland, the people once again became careless and apathetic in their spirituality. While they had not yet fully rebelled against God, their disobedience was leading them in that direction. They had begun disobeying the Law and were not giving God their best, whether in time, finances, marital relationships, or worship practices. It was then that Malachi arrived on the scene, encouraging the people to give only their best to God.

Purpose and Themes

Identify the major themes of the Book of Malachi.

The purpose of the Book of Malachi was to call people back to covenant faithfulness. He wanted to reemphasize God’s sovereignty over the people and their need to relate to Him faithfully as laid out in the covenant (Hill and Walton 2023, 499–500).

Outline

The Book of Malachi is structured around six disputes or debates between the people of Israel and God. Even the priests sometimes argued with God. These disputes focus on ten questions raised by the people in response to God’s charges against them. Further, “the six disputations center on God’s nature” (Brubaker 2003a, 834).

I. First Message: God Has Loved Israel (1:2–5)

Question 1: How have You loved us? (1:2)

II. Second Message: Israel Has Dishonored God (1:6–2:9)

Question 2: How have we shown contempt for Your name? (1:6)

Question 3: How have we defiled You? (1:7)

III. Third Message: Judah Has Been Unfaithful in Two Ways (2:10–16)

Question 4: Why does the Lord not pay attention to our offerings? (2:14)

IV. Fourth Message: The Lord Will Come Suddenly (2:17–3:6)

Question 5: How have we wearied Him? (2:17)

Question 6: Where is the God of justice? (2:17)

V. Fifth Message: Return to the Lord (3:7–12)

Question 7: How are we to return? (3:7)

Question 8: How do we rob You? (3:8)

VI. Sixth Message: Israel Has Said Harsh Things Against God (3:13–18)

Question 9: What have we said against You? (3:13)

Question 10: What did we gain by carrying out Your requirements? (3:14)

VII. Seventh Message: The Day of the Lord Is Coming (4:1–6)

Content

In reading Malachi, remember that in spite of the people’s recent deliverance from exile, their current conditions looked negative and bleak to them. Although they had worked hard with good motivation, they had encountered opposition and great difficulty in rebuilding the temple and the walls of Jerusalem. To them, this meant that they had somehow displeased the Lord and were reaping the consequences. As a result, the people had grown cynical: If this was the way God was going to treat them, why should they bother giving their best to God? Yet negative circumstances in life are not necessarily a result of God’s discipline or judgment. They sometimes simply result from living in a fallen world.

God responded to His people’s questions by assuring them of His love and compassion for them. Still, He did have some charges to bring against them. For instance, although the people acknowledged that God was their “King” and “Lord,” they brought unhealthy animals to sacrifice in violation of the God-given law. Their actions did not match their words and therefore demonstrated insincerity. God desired that they bring their very best because He is worthy of respect, reverence, and devotion.

Other issues involved marriage. Some of the people were marrying outside the faith and being led into idolatry by their spouses. In other cases, when a couple became older, the husband divorced the wife of his youth and married someone he perceived as younger and prettier. Divorce became rampant for unlawful reasons. It is in this context that God stated through the prophet, “I hate divorce” (Malachi 2:16). While other biblical passages discuss divorce and remarriage from different perspectives, in this passage God declares His detachment from divorce without offering further qualifications. Nevertheless, God’s ideal is lifetime commitment in marriage.

Another charge involved the giving of finances to God (Malachi 3). The law of Moses instructed the people to give God 10 percent of their income—a tithe. The tithe was to remind them that everything they had was from God’s hand. However, the people were failing to bring their tithe.

Once again, Malachi called attention to the Lord’s consistent attributes: God is compassionate, changeless, the true King and Lord of all, and Creator of all, and He showers blessings on us. Malachi exhorted the people to shake off their apathy and carelessness; appreciate God’s numerous gifts (including deliverance out of the Exile); and sacrifice the very best in time, finances, worship, and service to the King of kings and Lord of lords—giving Him the very best.

The book closes with a famous prophecy about Elijah. Hill and Walton explain the significance of this prophecy for the New Testament:

Elijah’s role as a herald proclaiming the appointed time of Yahweh’s wrath and the inauguration of the messianic age was an important part of later Jewish tradition regarding the prophet (e.g., Sir. 48:10–11; cf. Matt. 17:3, 10; 27:47, 49; John 1:21). His appearance on the Mount of Transfiguration with Moses and Jesus lends support to this eschatological dimension of the prophetic ministry. Jesus of Nazareth clearly understood the prophecy of Malachi as prefiguring the ministry of John the Baptist (Matt. 11:7–15). The New Testament record also testifies to the unconventional, Elijah-like life and ministry of John (e.g., Matt. 3:1–12; Luke 1:14–17). The early church also accepted Malachi’s prediction about the “forerunner” as being fully realized in John’s heralding the initiation of the messianic kingdom of heaven accomplished by the advent of Jesus as the Christ (Mark 1:2–8; Luke 1:16–17; cf. Matt. 1:1–6). (Hill and Walton 2023, 503)

**Q:** What internal biblical evidence points to the time period when Malachi was written?

**Q:** What are the purpose and prominent themes of Malachi?

**A:** The purpose is to call God’s people back to spiritual vitality. The first theme emphasizes the nature and attributes of God, a second theme is the keeping of the covenant, and a third theme is the giving of one’s best to God.

**Q:** How does the end of Malachi anticipate the events of the New Testament?

#### 14.3.2 — Identify the major themes of the Book of Malachi.

Discuss the setting and time of Malachi’s ministry.

Author and Date

Because the word Malachi means “my messenger,” some scholars assert that this is not a proper name and that the authorship of Malachi is anonymous. Hill and Walton note, “Malachi is similar to other Old Testament names ending in i, such as Beeri (Gen. 26:34) and Zichri (1 Chron. 8:19). Moreover, both Jonah and Habakkuk are solitary names among the prophets. So this single occurrence of the name Malachi should not count as evidence against its use as a proper name” (Hill and Walton 2009, 702).

Malachi keeps the focus on God’s message and away from the messenger (himself). Therefore, we do not know much about this prophet other than that he lived in Jerusalem. While the book itself does not refer to particular historical dates or events, the internal biblical evidence points strongly to a date after the Exile. This evidence includes references to a governor, a temple, and similar social conditions to the age of Ezra and Nehemiah. In fact, some references and similarities indicate that Malachi may have been a contemporary of Nehemiah. However, some Bible scholars suggest that because Ezra and Nehemiah are not specifically mentioned in the book, Malachi’s prophetic ministry may have occurred before they came to Judah (Brubaker 2003a, 833). Due to this uncertainty, we cannot pin the date of Malachi’s writing to a specific date, but we can surmise that Malachi wrote his book sometime in the range of 470–420 BC.

Setting

As with Haggai and Zechariah, the historical background of Malachi involves the political, social, and religious conditions that existed after the Exile. Having returned and settled into their homeland, the people once again became careless and apathetic in their spirituality. While they had not yet fully rebelled against God, their disobedience was leading them in that direction. They had begun disobeying the Law and were not giving God their best, whether in time, finances, marital relationships, or worship practices. It was then that Malachi arrived on the scene, encouraging the people to give only their best to God.

Purpose and Themes

Identify the major themes of the Book of Malachi.

The purpose of the Book of Malachi was to call people back to covenant faithfulness. He wanted to reemphasize God’s sovereignty over the people and their need to relate to Him faithfully as laid out in the covenant (Hill and Walton 2023, 499–500).

Outline

The Book of Malachi is structured around six disputes or debates between the people of Israel and God. Even the priests sometimes argued with God. These disputes focus on ten questions raised by the people in response to God’s charges against them. Further, “the six disputations center on God’s nature” (Brubaker 2003a, 834).

I. First Message: God Has Loved Israel (1:2–5)

Question 1: How have You loved us? (1:2)

II. Second Message: Israel Has Dishonored God (1:6–2:9)

Question 2: How have we shown contempt for Your name? (1:6)

Question 3: How have we defiled You? (1:7)

III. Third Message: Judah Has Been Unfaithful in Two Ways (2:10–16)

Question 4: Why does the Lord not pay attention to our offerings? (2:14)

IV. Fourth Message: The Lord Will Come Suddenly (2:17–3:6)

Question 5: How have we wearied Him? (2:17)

Question 6: Where is the God of justice? (2:17)

V. Fifth Message: Return to the Lord (3:7–12)

Question 7: How are we to return? (3:7)

Question 8: How do we rob You? (3:8)

VI. Sixth Message: Israel Has Said Harsh Things Against God (3:13–18)

Question 9: What have we said against You? (3:13)

Question 10: What did we gain by carrying out Your requirements? (3:14)

VII. Seventh Message: The Day of the Lord Is Coming (4:1–6)

Content

In reading Malachi, remember that in spite of the people’s recent deliverance from exile, their current conditions looked negative and bleak to them. Although they had worked hard with good motivation, they had encountered opposition and great difficulty in rebuilding the temple and the walls of Jerusalem. To them, this meant that they had somehow displeased the Lord and were reaping the consequences. As a result, the people had grown cynical: If this was the way God was going to treat them, why should they bother giving their best to God? Yet negative circumstances in life are not necessarily a result of God’s discipline or judgment. They sometimes simply result from living in a fallen world.

God responded to His people’s questions by assuring them of His love and compassion for them. Still, He did have some charges to bring against them. For instance, although the people acknowledged that God was their “King” and “Lord,” they brought unhealthy animals to sacrifice in violation of the God-given law. Their actions did not match their words and therefore demonstrated insincerity. God desired that they bring their very best because He is worthy of respect, reverence, and devotion.

Other issues involved marriage. Some of the people were marrying outside the faith and being led into idolatry by their spouses. In other cases, when a couple became older, the husband divorced the wife of his youth and married someone he perceived as younger and prettier. Divorce became rampant for unlawful reasons. It is in this context that God stated through the prophet, “I hate divorce” (Malachi 2:16). While other biblical passages discuss divorce and remarriage from different perspectives, in this passage God declares His detachment from divorce without offering further qualifications. Nevertheless, God’s ideal is lifetime commitment in marriage.

Another charge involved the giving of finances to God (Malachi 3). The law of Moses instructed the people to give God 10 percent of their income—a tithe. The tithe was to remind them that everything they had was from God’s hand. However, the people were failing to bring their tithe.

Once again, Malachi called attention to the Lord’s consistent attributes: God is compassionate, changeless, the true King and Lord of all, and Creator of all, and He showers blessings on us. Malachi exhorted the people to shake off their apathy and carelessness; appreciate God’s numerous gifts (including deliverance out of the Exile); and sacrifice the very best in time, finances, worship, and service to the King of kings and Lord of lords—giving Him the very best.

The book closes with a famous prophecy about Elijah. Hill and Walton explain the significance of this prophecy for the New Testament:

Elijah’s role as a herald proclaiming the appointed time of Yahweh’s wrath and the inauguration of the messianic age was an important part of later Jewish tradition regarding the prophet (e.g., Sir. 48:10–11; cf. Matt. 17:3, 10; 27:47, 49; John 1:21). His appearance on the Mount of Transfiguration with Moses and Jesus lends support to this eschatological dimension of the prophetic ministry. Jesus of Nazareth clearly understood the prophecy of Malachi as prefiguring the ministry of John the Baptist (Matt. 11:7–15). The New Testament record also testifies to the unconventional, Elijah-like life and ministry of John (e.g., Matt. 3:1–12; Luke 1:14–17). The early church also accepted Malachi’s prediction about the “forerunner” as being fully realized in John’s heralding the initiation of the messianic kingdom of heaven accomplished by the advent of Jesus as the Christ (Mark 1:2–8; Luke 1:16–17; cf. Matt. 1:1–6). (Hill and Walton 2023, 503)

**Q:** What internal biblical evidence points to the time period when Malachi was written?

**Q:** What are the purpose and prominent themes of Malachi?

**A:** The purpose is to call God’s people back to spiritual vitality. The first theme emphasizes the nature and attributes of God, a second theme is the keeping of the covenant, and a third theme is the giving of one’s best to God.

**Q:** How does the end of Malachi anticipate the events of the New Testament?

## 15 - The Connection (The Old and New Testaments)

### The Discontinuity

#### 15.1.1 — Explain the differences between the Old and New Testaments.

Explain the differences between the Old and New Testaments.

Both continuity (similarities) and discontinuity (differences) exist between the Old and New Testaments. While it is important to acknowledge aspects of the New Testament that are genuinely distinct from the Old Testament, it is critical to emphasize the substantial points of continuity between the Old and New Testaments. All of the New Testament authors and early Christians affirmed that the Old Testament spoke of Jesus. We must affirm the same.

In this lesson, we will consider the discontinuity between the testaments. Although space will not allow us to enumerate all the distinctions, the following are four significant differences:

The political and historical contexts during Old Testament times differed from the political and historical contexts of New Testament times. Many pastors and popular influencers draw strict parallels between ancient political powers and today’s modern nation-state system. This approach creates problematic readings of the Old Testament.

The Old Testament ends with the impression that this is an unfinished story. The Messiah has not yet come, and the cycles of sin and spiritual revitalization, of oppression and deliverance, continue. The New Testament begins with the first coming of the Messiah and ends with His promised return.

Old Testament Israelites are considered the revelatory people of God, and New Testament believers are the soteriological people of God (Hill and Walton 2000, 568). That is, God revealed His covenant plan, stipulations, and commands—His law—through the nation of Israel, His revelatory people. Yet in the New Testament, God brought grace and salvation to individuals, meaning that believers are God’s soteriological people. ( Soteriology is the theological study of salvation.) This does not infer that an individual relationship with God was unimportant in the Old Testament. It simply means that the primary purpose of God’s revelation was national in the Old Testament and individual in the New Testament.

Somewhat related to the other distinctions is the difference in the sacrificial requirements of the two testaments or covenants. In the Old Testament, as the people looked forward to the Messiah’s coming, God instituted an annual sacrifice of a lamb to atone for the people’s sins. They were also to make other offerings and sacrifices as the Law commanded. However, in the New Testament, the Messiah and Son of God, Jesus Christ, perfect God and perfect man, atoned for the sins of humankind once and for all (Hebrews 10:1–18). Interestingly, God exercised both His love and His wrath when Jesus Christ substituted himself for every sinner in every age in every nation. The atonement of His shed blood covers our every sin so that we are forgiven and never have to offer animal sacrifices again. How could the believer understand the significance of the Messiah’s actions if not for the Old Testament’s unifying themes of atonement and sacrifice?

In addition to these differences, many perceive a sharp contrast between the Old Testament God of wrath and the New Testament God of love. This claim creates a false dichotomy, however. As we have seen throughout this course, the God of the Old Testament does exercise judgment and punishment, but He is unmistakably a God of love. The frequent use of family and marriage metaphors throughout the Old Testament emphasize God’s love for His people. Likewise, while the New Testament refers to God as a God of love, He is still the God who judges the nations and people for their sins. Ananias and Sapphira experience judgment for their sin (Acts 5:1–11), and the Book of Revelation depicts God in ways that are reminiscent of His depiction in the Old Testament prophets.

Another perceived difference is Old Testament law versus New Testament grace, or Old Testament law versus New Testament gospel. But is the distinction between law and grace as pronounced as some contend? While it is true that Scripture presents a law–gospel tension, it also presents the Law and the gospel as complementary. Upon close inspection, we see that the Old Testament has many illustrations of God’s patience, mercy, and grace and the New Testament includes a number of references to divine judgment. God’s mercy and God’s judgment are seen in both Testaments.

Therefore, it is best to acknowledge that there is both diversity and unity in Scripture just as there is both diversity and unity in the body of Christ. This discontinuity or diversity between the testaments was God’s intention; thus, it does not in any way conflict with His consistent, unchanging nature.

**Q:** What are some significant differences (discontinuity) between the Old and New Testaments?

**Q:** What is meant by revelatory people and soteriological people?

**A:** a) the Day of the Lord and the coming Messiah. b) Babylon’s destruction and the Day of the Lord. c) God’s judgment of sin in His time and living by faith. d) the Day of the Lord and living by faith.

### The Continuity

#### 15.2.1 — Explain the similarities and unity between the Old and New Testaments.

Explain the similarities and unity between the Old and New Testaments.

The concept of continuity means that the Scriptures are unified in their divine intent and purpose. That is, the Old and New Testaments are connected in some way. What things do they have in common? How are they similar? What themes tie the two testaments together? How did Jesus and the New Testament authors view the Old Testament?

Perhaps we could describe the canon of Scripture at the end of the Old Testament as the end of “act one” of a two-act play. . . . The plot has been established: A fallen human race needs redemption and reconciliation. The key players have been introduced: a holy God and his chosen people. . . . But the tension of the story remains unresolved. God’s people are back in their own land, but they are a subject nation. What happened to visions of national grandeur? There is a sense of unfulfilled expectations: Where is that final messianic figure who will break the chains of sin and usher in a new age of peace? . . . It would be four hundred years between Malachi and Matthew. History doesn’t stop. Kings and kingdoms came and went. But God’s redemptive plan stayed on schedule. When the time was right, Jesus would bring salvation to both Jews and Gentiles (Galatians 4:4). God’s kingdom arrived in the person of his Son. (Brubaker 2003a, 836–837)

This dynamic redemptive and historical context highlights some of the unifying themes of the Old and New Testaments. First, Brubaker’s two-act play imagery illustrates what scholars call salvation history . In other words, the testaments tell the history of God’s redemptive actions, promises, and fulfillments. In relation to salvation history, we can make two observations:

1. The Bible has a promise-fulfillment motif that occurs throughout the canon. In the Old Testament, prophecies are frequently fulfilled, but there are many promises that remain unfulfilled, especially those related to the Messiah. The New Testament authors repeatedly highlight how Christ fulfilled the Old Testament prophecies (for example, Matthew 1:1–6; Luke 3:23–28). In this sense, the New Testament is the fulfillment of that which the Old Testament anticipated.

2. The Old and New Testaments have an integrated and codependent relationship. Christian tradition affirms that you cannot read either testament in exclusion of the other. In other words, you cannot rightly understand the Old Testament without the New Testament, and you cannot rightly understand the New Testament without the Old Testament.

Yet the scope of the testaments’ relationship extends further than just these first two aspects. The Old and New Testaments have an incredible literary relationship. Not only does the New Testament contain a significant number of quotations from and allusions to the Old Testament, but also literary devices and language techniques used by the New Testament authors appear to be strongly influenced by the Old Testament. “One scholar has counted 295 separate explicit references to the Old Testament, occupying some 352 verses of the New” (Arnold and Beyer 1999, 476). “The New Testament made extensive use of the Old Testament in the form of direct quotation and indirect allusion. In fact, according to careful calculation, approximately 32 percent—nearly one-third—of the New Testament is composed of Old Testament quotations and allusions” (Hill and Walton 2023, 532).

As we have already suggested, the Old Testament is undoubtedly the historical basis for the New Testament. No contemporary reader could truly understand the New Testament’s themes and purposes without the historical information and background described in the Old Testament. With that in mind, we can affirm that the Old Testament is also the theological foundation of the New Testament. Many theological themes on which biblical doctrines are based are first introduced and sometimes explained in the Old Testament. The New Testament expands on these themes and explains them in terms of their fulfillment. In some cases, New Testament authors assumed that major themes of Scripture (such as creation, sin, and redemption) were already familiar to their readers because of the readers’ acquaintance with the Old Testament. Among the themes emphasized in both testaments are (1) God’s sovereignty, (2) God as the one true God, (3) God’s holiness, (4) God’s transcendence , (5) God’s righteousness and justice, (6) God’s compassion and grace, (7) the covenant, and (8) God’s loyalty.

**Q:** What is the promise fulfillment motif between the testaments?

**Q:** What is the historical connection between the Old and New Testaments?

**A:** a) Judah. b) Moab. c) Israel. d) Edom.

**Q:** Name some themes that are emphasized in both testaments.

**A:** a) Israel. b) Nineveh. c) Judah. d) Edom.

### The Relevance

#### 15.3.1 — List principles for evaluating how to determine whether an Old Testament passage applies to believers today.

List principles for evaluating how to determine whether an Old Testament passage applies to believers today.

Is the Old Testament truly relevant to our lives today? Throughout Church history, Christians have maintained that the Old Testament does remain relevant for the believer. Unfortunately, many Christians throughout history have made the critical error of omitting the Old Testament from serious study. When this has been done intentionally, it has generated horrific results, including the antisemitism of the Holocaust. While unintentionally omitting the Old Testament is a more common mistake, it poses similar problems because it distorts the gospel and disconnects Jesus from His Jewish heritage. The question for Christians then is not whether we should read the Old Testament, but how should we relate to it.

Thus, both the continuity and discontinuity of the Old and New Testaments must be discussed in terms of basic principles of interpretation (a discipline called hermeneutics ). We must understand which laws or concepts were meant to be universal and which were uniquely designed for the intended theocracy of Israel under the old covenant. As we have noted, New Testament believers are not to sacrifice animals or consider such laws as a divine command for today. But why not? How do we determine whether an Old Testament passage is prescriptive for our times? What principles of biblical interpretation (hermeneutics) should we use? Following are three principles to use as guidelines:

Apply the principles to an assigned Old Testament passage, and state whether that particular passage is applicable today. Descriptive versus prescriptive: The Old and New Testaments include both descriptive and prescriptive passages. That is, some passages are intended only to describe behavior, while others are meant to prescribe or encourage certain behavior or actions. For instance, while the intent of Old Testament narrative is to teach both history and theology, the biblical record of Israel’s conquest of Canaan is not meant to be a divine prescription for believers to conquer any new land they deem given to them by God. Instead, Old Testament narratives frequently teach a universal principle that can be extracted from the narrative. Additionally, some Old Testament narratives, like David’s sin against Uriah and Bathsheba, are meant to warn us about the dangers of yielding to temptation and lying.

Apply the principles to an assigned Old Testament passage, and state whether that particular passage is applicable today.

Noting the context: According to this principle, readers or interpreters should pay attention to the immediate context of the phrase in question; they should look at the surrounding words to better understand the statement’s meaning. Readers should then consider the context of the verse, the chapter, and the entire book of the Bible. Further, they should determine whether a New Testament passage interprets an Old Testament passage. Since the New Testament so frequently appeals to the Old Testament, readers should be alert to the ways that Christ’s ministry, life, death, and resurrection reinterpret Old Testament passages. As Jesus famously insisted on the road to Emmaus, all of the Scriptures ultimately speak of Him and His role in God’s redemption of the world.

Principles regarding Old Testament law: The Old Testament law was designed for the nation of Israel in the context of God’s intended theocracy and the multitude’s constant traveling and camping. Many of the laws were for the protection of their health in these unique circumstances. As a result, some scholars advocate a principle of distinguishing between the Old Testament’s civil and ceremonial laws and its moral laws. Others propose a related principle that only the laws that are renewed in the New Testament are prescriptive or authoritative for the believer today. Applying these two principles in harmony solves some of the perplexing issues raised in certain Old Testament passages. That is, since Christ has fulfilled the ceremonial laws, and the civil laws were intended for Israel’s unique context, the civil and ceremonial laws do not apply to today’s believers. However, the moral laws such as the Ten Commandments still apply (are prescriptive) because of their moral nature and because most of them were renewed in the New Testament.

In regard to the Ten Commandments, some may note a discrepancy in the days set aside for the Sabbath. Although Saturday was the original Sabbath Day as established in the Old Testament, many hold that the New Testament believers changed the day of worship to Sunday to commemorate Jesus’ resurrection on the first day of the week. In addition, Paul instructed believers to bring their offerings on the first day of the week: Sunday. In any case, the principle of setting one day aside during the week for worship and rest is continued in the New Testament.

In sum, the Old Testament—in its entirety—is not intended to be normative or authoritative for the believer today. Contending that all of the Old Testament is prescriptive leads to legalism, which is condemned in the New Testament. At the same time, it is wrong to conclude that none of the Old Testament is normative or authoritative for us. To do so is to destroy many of the Old Testament’s foundations and explanations of biblically balanced doctrines. The correct view lies somewhere between the two extremes. Old Testament scholar John Bright explains,

That the Old Testament cannot be, in all its parts, directly normative for the Christian is obvious. It contains much—ancient laws and customs, ancient institutions and ways of thinking—that cannot serve as a model for Christian faith and practice, as well as attitudes and actions that are not safe guides for the Christian conscience to follow. . . . But—and it cannot be repeated too often—this line must not be drawn in such a way that we are betrayed once again into a selective or one sided use of the Old Testament. The problem of the Old Testament is not to be solved by subtraction. (Bright 1991, 141)

Believers should understand the discontinuity and continuity between the testaments without overemphasizing either aspect. Both testaments are authoritative for the believer when proper hermeneutical principles are applied to determine correct interpretations of biblical passages.

Modern-day believers must recognize the Old Testament as the inspired Word of God, just like the New Testament. Jesus and the New Testament authors frequently quoted and alluded to Old Testament passages, events, and literary vocabulary. When they use the word Scriptures , they are often referring to the Old Testament and not the documents that became the New Testament. The Old Testament opens the pages of history, sees God’s sovereign hand at work through the actions of peoples and nations, traces the history of redemption, and introduces the promises that are fulfilled in the New Testament—including the numerous prophecies of the Messiah, Jesus Christ.

The continuity of the two testaments, as well as Jesus’ life, death, and resurrection, help believers more fully understand the Old Testament and appreciate God’s forgiveness, compassion, grace, and sovereignty. The message of both testaments is reflected in the words of a well-known hymn: “To God be the glory, great things He hath done. So loved He the world, that He gave us His Son.” With a clear understanding of the Old Testament in relation to the New Testament, let us declare this glory of God among the nations!

**Q:** What is the descriptive/prescriptive principle?

**A:** a) Israel’s sin. b) the fall of Judah and Jerusalem. c) Israel’s future. d) Israel’s coming judgment.

**Q:** How should a reader apply the hermeneutics principle that notes the context?

**A:** a) wrath against unrepentant sin. b) peace. c) righteousness. d) faithfulness and unfailing love.

The continuity of the two testaments, as well as Jesus’ life, death, and resurrection, help believers more fully understand the Old Testament and appreciate God’s forgiveness, compassion, grace, and sovereignty. The message of both testaments is reflected in the words of a well-known hymn: “To God be the glory, great things He hath done. So loved He the world, that He gave us His Son.” With a clear understanding of the Old Testament in relation to the New Testament, let us declare this glory of God among the nations!

**Q:** What is the descriptive/prescriptive principle?

**A:** a) Israel’s sin. b) the fall of Judah and Jerusalem. c) Israel’s future. d) Israel’s coming judgment.

**Q:** How should a reader apply the hermeneutics principle that notes the context?

**A:** a) wrath against unrepentant sin. b) peace. c) righteousness. d) faithfulness and unfailing love.
